# How functions are written and ordered: literature and source notes

Compiled 2026-10-29 for the Ultracite research post on function style and order. The rules: ESLint's `func-style` and `no-use-before-define` (and `@typescript-eslint/no-use-before-define`), with their neighbours `func-names`, `func-name-matching`, `prefer-arrow-callback`, `arrow-body-style`, `no-inner-declarations` and `no-func-assign`; Oxlint's ports; Biome's `useArrowFunction`, `noInvalidUseBeforeDeclaration`, `useConsistentArrowReturn`, `noFunctionAssign`, `noInnerDeclarations` and its nursery `useConsistentFunctionStyle`; `react/function-component-definition`, `unicorn/consistent-function-scoping`, and the plugin rules that enforce one style or the other (`antfu/top-level-function`, `prefer-arrow-functions`). Ultracite sets `func-style` to `["error", "expression", { allowArrowFunctions: true }]` in Oxlint and ESLint and turns `no-use-before-define` on; Biome users get neither (section 9). The question: what each style actually changes (hoisting, the temporal dead zone, `this`, types, stack traces, file order), whether either style prevents bugs or helps readers, what forcing one style costs when code relies on the other, and what happens when agents are held to it. Module cycles and initialization order are in `import-cycles.md` (2.3–2.7); comprehension research in `cognitive-complexity.md` §3; splitting, comprehension and agents splitting code in `length-limits.md` (4.46–4.52, §5); method-ordering, review-order and navigation research in `sort-keys.md` (4.9–4.12). This file cross-references them rather than repeating them.

**How to read the tags**

- **VERIFIED**: the primary source (the spec, the PDF, the publisher or arXiv record, the official docs, the tool's source code or published package, the release notes, or the GitHub issue, PR or commit) was opened and the claim found in it.
- **VERIFIED (metadata + abstract)**: the bibliographic record (Crossref, OpenAlex, Semantic Scholar or the publisher page) and the abstract were checked, but not the full text.
- **ANECDOTAL**: a real, primary page exists and says what is reported here, but it is a single user report, project doc, blog post, tweet, maintainer's account or vendor document, not a study. Don't present it as a rate or a trend.
- **UNVERIFIED**: only secondary mentions were found. Don't cite these as fact.
- **Vendor** marks research or claims by a company that sells or owns a product in this space: Google, Microsoft/GitHub, Meta, Apple, Vercel, Anthropic, OpenAI, Anysphere (Cursor), JetBrains, Sonar, VoidZero/Oxc.

Every quote is copied exactly and is under 15 words. Where a longer sentence matters, it is given as consecutive quoted segments. Emphasis inside a quote is kept where the source has it (the formatter writes it as `_` or `**`); double quotes inside a quote are shown as single quotes. Every person is named or referred to as they/them.

**How sources were fetched.** Pages and PDFs were read with WebSearch, WebFetch (for finding pages only) and unauthenticated `curl` (`--max-filesize 10000000`), and every quote was copied from the saved raw text, not from a summary; PDFs were converted with `pdftotext`. Tool behaviour was read from source at pinned tags: ESLint `v10.13.0`, Oxlint `oxlint_v1.89.0`, Biome `@biomejs/biome@2.6.3` (`c5c3383d`), typescript-eslint `v8.72.0`, TypeScript `v6.0.3`, eslint-plugin-react `v7.37.5`, eslint-plugin-unicorn `v79.0.0`, JSHint `2.13.6`, esbuild `v0.28.2`, and the other tags and commits named in each entry. The current spec is the ECMAScript 2027 draft as served on 29 Oct 2026; older editions are Ecma's PDFs of ES1, ES3 and ES5. Metadata came from Crossref and OpenAlex (no `mailto`), Semantic Scholar (no key), the arXiv API, the npm registry and the Wayback Machine. Five research agents worked in parallel: section 1 and 6 (`A/`), section 2 (`B/`), section 3 (`C/`), sections 5 and 8 (`D/`), sections 4 and 7 (`E/`); section 9 and the presets are mine (`main/`). Every quote in this file was then checked by script against their saved texts and mine (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/scripts/check_quotes.py`, output in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/main/quote-check.txt`).

- **Blocked:** dl.acm.org and Wiley returned 403; Springer article pages and OpenEdition served bot challenges, and the UNLV repository a Cloudflare challenge (Wayback captures were used where they exist); O'Reilly's live pages need a login (Wayback captures of the previews were used); Semantic Scholar returned 429 on most searches and Crossref returned some empty responses; `gh search` on facebook/react returned 422. The 2021 BugsJS journal PDF (10.4 MB) and the ES2015 PDF (12.3 MB) were over the download limit and not fetched.
- **Books not opened:** _Clean Code_ chapters 3 and 5 (only the contents and index from Pearson's sample, plus one sentence as quoted by Geffen and Maoz), K&R's _The C Programming Language_, _JavaScript: The Good Parts_ beyond one previewed section of Appendix B, and Jensen and Wirth's Pascal _User Manual and Report_. No shadow library was used.
- **`gh` was used read-only** on public repositories: commits, files, issues, PRs, releases, code and issue search. No repository was cloned.
- **Nothing was installed.** The worktree's own Oxlint, ESLint, typescript-eslint, Biome, `tsc` and `tsgo` were run on small hand-written files in the scratchpad. No email address, name or account detail was put in any URL, query, header or form, and Unpaywall wasn't used (a Semantic Scholar response pointed to an Unpaywall URL, which was not followed). Nothing was left downloading.
- **Embedded instructions:** the Claude Code and OpenAI Codex docs pages open with notes telling agents to fetch an `llms.txt` index; a bot comment in Biome PR #12383 told AI agents to post a verification code on the thread; Vercel's React agent skill, the github/awesome-copilot instruction files and a community agent skill are instructions aimed at models. All were read as data and not followed. No source tried to direct the work.

**About the items tagged my check.** These were run on 29 Oct 2026 with the worktree's binaries (Oxlint 1.89.0, ESLint 10.13.0, typescript-eslint 8.72.0, Biome 2.6.3, TypeScript 6.0.3 and `tsgo` 7.0.0-dev.20260707.2, on Node 24.14.1; JavaScriptCore through Bun 1.4.4 in 1.12, 1.15 and 6.17). Each entry names its folder. The corpus measurements for this topic are being built separately in `research/corpus/results/func-style/`; nothing here depends on them. Move these checks into the harness under `--topic func-style` before putting a number in the post.

Hayden gave the reason for Ultracite's setting on 29 Oct 2026, relayed while this file was compiled; it is recorded as Hayden's account in 9.10 and in the notes, with the sources that bear on each part of it.

---

## Most useful for the post

1. **Hoisting was designed so functions can call each other in any order.** Eich (2011): "function declaration hoisting is for mutual recursion" and "generally to avoid painful bottom-up ML-like order"; (2014) "var hoisting was thus unintended consequence of function hoisting,". ES1 already said to "Process SourceElements for function declarations." before running any statement. (1.1, 1.4)
2. **The temporal dead zone was chosen so `const` functions could still call each other, and `no-use-before-define` is closer to the rule TC39 rejected.** Horwat (2008): "It's very useful, in that" "it allows mutually recursive lambdas."; HOPL: "A1 is undesirable because it prevents these declaration forms" "from being used to define mutually recursive functions." With the rule's defaults, two `const` arrows that call each other get one error whichever comes first, though the code runs; Standard, love and Node.js set `variables: false`, which allows that and still reports the same-scope read that would crash. (1.6, 1.10, 4.1, 4.8; my checks in the notes)
3. **Arrows were adopted for callbacks and lexical `this`, and name inference keeps `const` arrows named.** "The primary motivation for arrow functions was the frequent need" "to code verbose function expressions as call-back arguments"; "dynamic this binding is an oft-fired footgun."; by spec `const f = () => {}` gets `f.name === "f"`, and V8 and JavaScriptCore print that name in stack traces. Unnamed frames come from inline callbacks and member assignments. (1.5, 1.8, 1.12, 6.17)
4. **Concise bodies are the one thing only arrows have, and the one test of them found no effect.** In a JavaScript atoms-of-confusion experiment, the concise `x => x * x * x` got 54 of 70 correct answers and the block-bodied arrow 58 of 70 (McNemar p = 0.2891, not significant); it compared two arrows, not an arrow with `function`. JSLint has allowed arrows only with expression bodies since at least 2020. (5.20, 4.19)
5. **Typing a function through its variable is real, and other guides answer it with an exception, not a rule.** "Contextual typing occurs when the type of an expression is implied by its location."; the request to type declarations (#22063, open since 2018, 125 thumbs-up): "TypeScript is basically forcing the use of expressions, due to the issues described above."; Google's TS guide prefers declarations but "Arrow functions _may_ be used, for example, when an explicit type annotation is required."; ESLint added `allowTypeAnnotation` in 2025, and antfu's rule and eslint-config-webpack exempt typed variables. (2.2, 2.4, 2.20, 4.5, 4.12)
6. **But the forms TypeScript handles worse are on the `const` side.** Overloads need a declaration, and `func-style` skips them ("Overloaded function declarations are not reported as errors by this rule."); TS2775: "Assertions require every name in the call target" / "to be declared with an explicit type annotation."; TS2730: "An arrow function cannot have a 'this' parameter."; in `.tsx`, `<T>` is "ambiguous to parse"; a `never` helper only narrows if "each identifier in the function name references an entity with an explicit type,". (2.5, 2.6, 2.8, 2.9, 3.25)
7. **`func-style` began as two maintainers' opposite tastes, and every implementation since calls it a preference.** The 2013 proposal: Zakas "prefers FunctionDeclaration, and my personal preference is FunctionExpression,"; the docs: "There is really no correct or incorrect choice here, it is just a preference."; Biome's 2026 port, from an AI-written history: "This is a historical default, not evidence of a technical recommendation."; typescript-eslint's maintainer: "the declaration style doesn't matter". (3.7, 3.19, 2.20)
8. **No core linter converts declarations to `const` arrows, because the conversion changes behaviour.** ESLint: "in some cases replacing a function declaration with a function expression" "or vice-versa is not guaranteed to preserve the original semantics."; Unicorn: the forms "are not always semantic equivalents," "so `--fix` would be too risky for a style rule."; a plugin that does convert checks first, because "const removes hoisting: code above the declaration which eagerly uses the name" "would hit the TDZ". Deno's std converted its top-level arrows by hand in 2023 and broke `std/crypto`, problems that "aren't caught by the test suite." (3.10, 3.24, 4.9, 7.7)
9. **`no-use-before-define` is an ordering rule, not a crash detector.** "This rule is intentionally designed to be a little more opinionated" "than most of our rules."; Zakas: "arrow functions are lazily evaluated" "so any variable declared later in source order would be flagged."; in one test file, 1 of its 5 reports was a real crash, and Biome reported only that one. Biome's PR says what it gives up: "It cannot report cases where a function that uses a variable" "is called before the declaration of the variable." A crash through a callback passed every tool: "No lint error. Will error at runtime." (3.5, 3.18, 3.26, 3.27)
10. **`tsc` catches the direct crash and not the rest, which is why typescript-eslint dropped the rule from `recommended`.** In 20 hand-written cases, 10 threw and `tsc` 6.0.3 reported 4: a later declaration "can be legal if usage is deferred", and across files "order cannot be determined". 2020: "The errors caught by this rule are generally caught by typescript."; 2025, defending it: "This code will crash." The rule's defaults caught 8 of the 10 crashes and flagged 5 safe cases. (2.12, 2.13, 2.16, 2.17)
11. **No widely used config enforces `const` functions; the ones that enforce a style chose declarations.** Node.js core: `func-style` `declaration`, adopted because it was "the predominant style" ("77 instances of expressions to 2344 instances of" declarations) and "to avoid potential confusion over the Temporal Dead Zone"; ESLint's own config: `["error", "declaration"]`; antfu: "Top-level functions should be declared with function keyword"; Airbnb's `func-style` has been `'off'` with "TODO: enable" since 2016; love files it under "too much". (4.1, 4.2, 4.10, 4.12, 4.15, 4.16, 7.4)
12. **The guides cited for expressions don't say what they're cited for.** Airbnb's guide says "Use named function expressions instead of function declarations.", but its maintainer: "Basically any time a function isn’t an inline callback," "it should be a traditional function." and "Components should never be arrow functions,"; Google's TS guide: "Prefer function declarations over arrow functions or function expressions when" "defining named functions." (4.2, 4.3, 4.5)
13. **The case against hoisting is old, and it's about order, not syntax.** Crockford, 2008: hoisting "relaxes the requirement that functions should be declared before used," "which I think leads to sloppiness."; Crockford's conventions: "All functions should be declared before they are used.", written with declarations. Airbnb's maintainer: "function declarations with the `no-use-before-define` eslint rule also avoid relying on hoisting,"; ESLint's own codebase does exactly that. (4.4, 4.16, 4.18, 4.20)
14. **Framework docs write components as declarations, and Ultracite's React settings reject them.** react.dev's learn pages have 1,051 declaration lines and 0 arrow components, Next.js's getting-started docs 221 and 0; `export default function Page()` gets "Function component is not an arrow function" from Oxlint and ESLint, with no fix; React's `const Greeting = memo(function Greeting({ name }) {` fails `prefer-arrow-callback`; in three TanStack Router docs pages, all 42 route components are `function` declarations below the route. (4.17, 3.21, 9.4, 9.6)
15. **The one experiment on method order found a time effect for beginners, no correctness effect, and no test of direction.** "while method ordering has no clear effect on the correctness of comprehension," "it does seem to have an effect on the time engineers spent"; "more experience implies less sensitivity to method order"; "too few samples to consider statistical significance". Callers-first was the most common order in 100,600 Java classes, but no condition put callees first. (5.8)
16. **What drives reading is how far the callee sits from its call, not which side it's on, and neither convention rests on evidence.** "the linearity of source code has an even stronger effect on reading order" "than experience", measured as an absolute distance; Clean Code: "a function that is called should be below a function that does the calling" (as quoted by Geffen and Maoz); Pascal's define-before-use came with a one-pass compiler; Kotlin: "Choose an order (either higher-level stuff first, or vice versa)" "and stick to it." Ultracite's two rules force callees first, the reverse of the stepdown rule. (5.1, 5.4, 5.7, 5.9, 5.10)
17. **No study compares `function` declarations with `const` arrows; the nearest results are small and from other languages.** C++ lambdas against iterators: "there is a slight, statistically significant, negative impact on the amount" of time, for students only; Java lambdas against anonymous classes: "the effect seems to be so small,". (5.16, 5.18, 5.22)
18. **No bug study has a hoisting or TDZ category, and `this` bugs are rare where measured.** BugsJS: "Incorrect this bounding" in 0 of 453 bugs; Ocariza et al. (2013): "Undefined Method" in 15 of 317, not split by cause; Hanam et al. list "this not correctly bound" as one of 13 patterns. (6.6–6.10)
19. **Crashes come from turning a hoisted binding into a `const`, and tools make that change too.** Next.js #58778: arrow exports threw "ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization"; "after converting to a function declaration the error goes away." React Compiler "lowers `parseItem` to a `const` while leaving `useState(getItem)` above it." In Ultracite's own history a `no-var` autofix produced "a self-referencing `const` — a guaranteed TDZ error at module load." All single reports. (6.1, 6.2, 9.5)
20. **The runtime differences are small and specific.** V8 engineers: closed-over uses "cannot have their hole checks elided, because of function declaration hoisting."; in Node 24 bytecode a call to a `const` arrow carries a TDZ check and a call to a declaration doesn't; esbuild turns top-level `const` into `var` when bundling ("there is no way to turn it off."), so a too-early call becomes a `TypeError`; no engine source shows a speed or memory difference between the styles. (6.5, 6.13, 6.16)
21. **There is usage data on arrows and almost none on preference.** State of JS asked only whether people had used arrows (22,226 of 22,695, 97.9%, in 2020); in 9,548 ESLint configs from 2017, `func-style` was the 14th most-disabled rule among projects without a preset (549 of 3,135). (7.1–7.3)
22. **Nobody has measured what agents write, and vendor guidance leans the other way.** No study measures arrows against declarations in model output, or agents converting between them; Vercel's React agent skill has 149 declaration lines against 19 `const` arrows (my count); GitHub's example says "Use arrow functions for callbacks and short functions" next to a `function` declaration, the same sentence Ultracite's rules file uses; vendor prompts say "Keep changes consistent with the style of the existing codebase." and "do not fix pre-existing errors on lines you didn't touch." (8.3–8.5, 8.7, 8.8, 8.14, 9.8)
23. **Ultracite: a preference since the first commit, copied between linters, with Hayden's reasons now on record.** `func-style` has been "expression" since March 2022 with no comment; Biome users have never had it; `allowArrowFunctions: true` does nothing in "expression" mode, and ESLint plans to reject that config ("will not accept the config that has no effect on the linting."); the only written reasoning is the TanStack override, whose `tsc` backstop covers the direct case only. Hayden: "i preferred function expressions because they allow for dropping curly braces where appropriate" "but honestly it was a personal preference." (9.1, 9.4, 9.6, 9.10, 3.9)

---

## 1. What the language does, from the spec, and its history

Items 1.1–1.3 are the early editions of ECMA-262 (ES1, ES3, ES5) on how function declarations were set up before code ran. 1.4–1.5 are Brendan Eich's own account (two tweets, and the HOPL paper with Allen Wirfs-Brock). 1.6–1.7 are the temporal dead zone decision and the 2012 fight over its cost. 1.8 is the arrow function proposal from strawman to TC39 consensus. 1.9–1.15 are the current spec draft: how declarations, `var`, `let`/`const` and blocks are instantiated, name inference, what arrows lack, strict mode, and modules (the module cycle details are in `import-cycles.md` 2.3–2.4 and not repeated). The current spec is saved as `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/ecma262-draft.html` (ECMAScript 2027 draft, `last-modified` 30 Oct 2026 00:35 GMT, 7.6 MB) with a plain-text copy in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt`; the old editions and the HOPL paper are in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/`; TC39 notes are in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/tc39-notes/` at tc39/notes `53a59ad7`. Spec section numbers move between editions; cite by name. The small checks I ran are in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/check/` with output in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/`.

### 1.1 ES1 (June 1997): function declarations only at the top level, created before any statement runs. VERIFIED (Ecma PDF)

- **Citation:** Ecma International, _ECMA-262, 1st edition_, June 1997. https://www.ecma-international.org/wp-content/uploads/ECMA-262_1st_edition_june_1997.pdf
- **What it says:**
  - §10.1.3 Variable instantiation: on entering an execution context, "For each FunctionDeclaration in the code, in source text order," a function object is created and stored on the variable object. Variables come next, set to `undefined`, and if a variable has the same name as a declared function, "the variable declaration does not disturb the existing property."
  - Function declarations could only appear in global (and eval) code: "Function declarations in other types of code are not allowed by the grammar." The §13 grammar is `function Identifier ( FormalParameterList ) Block`, and a `Block` holds only statements, so a function body could not contain a nested declaration.
  - §14 Program: step 1 is "Process SourceElements for function declarations." and only step 2 evaluates them.
  - ES1 has no `FunctionExpression` production at all (0 matches in the text; the same in ES2, August 1998).
- **Caveat:** ES1 standardised Netscape's JavaScript 1.1-era language. Function expressions already existed in JavaScript 1.2 (1.5) before ES3 standardised them.
- **For the post:** "hoisting" was in the first standard from the start, as a two-pass rule: create every function, then run the code. Function declarations were the only kind of function, and only at the top level.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/ECMA-262_1st_edition_june_1997.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/ECMA-262_2nd_edition_august_1998.txt`

### 1.2 ES3 (December 1999): function expressions arrive; declarations stay out of blocks. VERIFIED (Ecma PDF; Horwat's 2008 note from the Ecma archive)

- **Citation:** Ecma International, _ECMA-262, 3rd edition_, December 1999. https://www.ecma-international.org/wp-content/uploads/ECMA-262_3rd_edition_december_1999.pdf ; Waldemar Horwat, "Substatement function definitions," message to the TC39-TG1 list, 21 Mar 2008, Ecma archive. https://www.ecma-international.org/archive/ecmascript/2008/misc/FibHist.pdf
- **What it says:**
  - §13 adds `FunctionExpression : function Identifier(opt) ( FormalParameterList(opt) ) { FunctionBody }` and `FunctionBody : SourceElements`, so functions can now nest.
  - §10.1.3: "On entering an execution context, the" "properties are bound to the variable object in the following order:" parameters, then each FunctionDeclaration "in source text order", then each `var` set to `undefined`.
  - The named-expression note, unchanged in today's spec (1.14): the name of a function expression "cannot be referenced from and does not affect the scope enclosing the" expression.
  - Why blocks were excluded, in Horwat's words: "I'm responsible for function declarations not being allowed in inner blocks" "or substatements within ES3." They "turned out to be conceptually harmful". Hoisting them "doesn't work because such functions" "can capture scopes that include variables that don't exist yet". For ES3.1, "they should be locally bound (as in point 2) and not hoisted."
- **Caveat:** Horwat's note is a 2008 recollection, written as a reply in a TC39 thread; the HOPL paper (1.5) reprints it. The archived PDF lists committee members' email addresses; don't reproduce them.
- **For the post:** ES3 gave JavaScript both forms. The committee already saw that hoisting a function that closes over variables which don't exist yet is a problem, which is the same problem the TDZ later turned into an error.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/ECMA-262_3rd_edition_december_1999.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/FibHist.txt`

### 1.3 ES5 (December 2009): block-level function declarations are non-portable, and `function` can't start an expression statement. VERIFIED (Ecma PDF)

- **Citation:** Ecma International, _ECMA-262, 5th edition_, December 2009, §12 (note), §12.4, §10.4.3. https://www.ecma-international.org/wp-content/uploads/ECMA-262_5th_edition_december_2009.pdf
- **What it says:**
  - §12 note: "Several widely used implementations of ECMAScript are known to support" "the use of FunctionDeclaration as a Statement." There are "significant and irreconcilable variations among the implementations", so "It is recommended that ECMAScript implementations" "either disallow this usage of FunctionDeclaration or issue a warning".
  - §12.4: `ExpressionStatement : [lookahead ∉ {{, function}] Expression ;`. An expression statement "cannot start with the function keyword because that might make it ambiguous" "with a FunctionDeclaration."
  - §10.4.3 (entering function code): "If the function code is strict code, set the ThisBinding to thisArg." Otherwise `null`/`undefined` become the global object. This is where a plain call's `this` became `undefined` in strict code.
- **Caveat:** ES5 left block functions undefined; ES2015 specified them (1.11).
- **For the post:** the grammar rule that `function` at the start of a statement is always a declaration is why `(function () {})()` needs parentheses and why a function expression is never hoisted.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/ECMA-262_5th_edition_december_2009.txt`

### 1.4 Brendan Eich on why function declarations hoist: mutual recursion and avoiding bottom-up order. VERIFIED (tweet text via X's oEmbed endpoint)

- **Citation:** Brendan Eich (@BrendanEich), reply to Dmitry Soshnikov and Jeremy Ashkenas, 4 Feb 2011, https://twitter.com/BrendanEich/status/33403701100154880 ; reply to @aravind030792, 15 Oct 2014, https://twitter.com/BrendanEich/status/522395336615428097 . Text fetched from `https://publish.twitter.com/oembed?url=...` (no login).
- **What it says:**
  - 2011: "yes, function declaration hoisting is for mutual recursion" and "generally to avoid painful bottom-up ML-like order".
  - 2014: "var hoisting was thus unintended consequence of function hoisting," "no block scope, JS as a 1995 rush job." and "ES6 'let' may help."
  - A 2008 es-discuss message by Eich (quoted inside Horwat's reply, 1.6) calls the case for `var` hoisting an "ancient, weak but not entirely worthless post-hoc rationale for var" hoisting, namely avoiding confusion among novice programmers.
- **Caveat:** tweets are short and were written 16–19 years after the design. They give the designer's stated intent, not a design document. The 2011 tweet is a "yes" to someone else's suggestion (Soshnikov's thread, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/soshnikov-note-4-hoisting.html`).
- **For the post:** the strongest primary answer to why hoisting exists: so functions can call each other in any order and files don't have to be written bottom-up. That is exactly what `no-use-before-define` plus `func-style: expression` takes away, so the post should say that Ultracite's choice reverses an explicit design goal, deliberately.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/eich-tweet-33403701100154880-oembed.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/eich-tweet-522395336615428097-oembed.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/horwat-2008-10-007807-wayback.txt`

### 1.5 Wirfs-Brock and Eich, "JavaScript: the first 20 years" (HOPL 2020): hoisting in JavaScript 1.0–1.2, block functions, arrows. VERIFIED (authors' corrected copy, March 2021, on Zenodo; Crossref)

- **Citation:** Allen Wirfs-Brock and Brendan Eich, "JavaScript: the first 20 years," _Proc. ACM Program. Lang._ 4, HOPL (June 2020), 189 pp., doi:10.1145/3386327 (CC BY 4.0). Corrected copy: https://zenodo.org/records/4960086 (`jshopl-corrected-2021-3.pdf`, 4.1 MB). The ACM PDF returned 403 to curl.
- **What it says:**
  - JavaScript 1.0/1.1: "functions could be defined only by global declarations" "at the top level of scripts." Forward references worked "because the language logically 'hoisted' top-level var and function declarations" to the start of the script.
  - JavaScript 1.2 (1997) added nested declarations, "also hoisted to the beginning of the enclosing function body", and function expressions, which are "syntactically identical to" declarations "except that the function name is optional."
  - ES3: "Function declarations were explicitly excluded from being nested" "within a {} block or as a substatement." Browsers implemented them anyway; "each implementation invented its own unique semantics for those" declarations, which "created significant problems for the designers of ES6".
  - Arrows (§21.3.5): "The primary motivation for arrow functions was the frequent need" "to code verbose function expressions as call-back arguments". Most of the committee "seemed interested primarily in concise function syntax rather than in Tennent's correspondence." Eich steered TC39 to the arrow design at the March 2012 meeting (1.8).
- **Caveat:** an insider history by two of the people involved; the 2021 corrected copy may differ in places from the ACM version.
- **For the post:** arrows were adopted for short callbacks and lexical `this`, not as a replacement for named top-level functions. That supports the maintainer's concise-body reason (1) for callbacks, but nothing in the design history argues for arrows as declarations.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/jshopl-corrected-2021-3.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/crossref-10.1145-3386327.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/zenodo-4960086.json`

### 1.6 The temporal dead zone was chosen so `let`/`const` could still define mutually recursive functions. VERIFIED (es-discuss via Wayback; HOPL)

- **Citation:** Waldemar Horwat, "return when desugaring to closures," es-discuss, 13 Oct 2008, https://mail.mozilla.org/pipermail/es-discuss/2008-October/007807.html (Wayback capture 3 Nov 2013); Wirfs-Brock and Eich (1.5), §21 on block scoping.
- **What it says:**
  - Horwat lists four options: "A1. Lexical dead zone.", "A2. Lexical window.", "B1. Temporal dead zone." ("References temporally prior to a definition in the same block are an error.") and "B2. Temporal window." Horwat credits Lars Hansen with the idea and argues B1 "matches the semantic model".
  - On why a dead zone at all, replying to Eich: "It's very useful, in that" "it allows mutually recursive lambdas."
  - HOPL: "A1 is undesirable because it prevents these declaration forms" "from being used to define mutually recursive functions." "It took nearly two years, but eventually" TC39 chose B1. The resulting rules: `let`, `const`, `class`, `import`, block-level function declarations and parameters "are dead at runtime until initialized", and "It is a runtime error to access or assign to an uninitialized binding."
- **Caveat:** the HOPL text calls the source "Horwat [2008a]" but its reference title is the thread name, which is easy to miss. The TC39 notes of 20 Sep 2012 have Eich dating the agreement to "2 years ago in redmond".
- **For the post:** the TDZ is the reason `const f = () => g()` and `const g = () => f()` work at all: a reference inside a function body is checked when it runs, not where it is written. A purely textual rule (A1) would have banned it. Ultracite's `no-use-before-define` is closer to A1 than to what the language chose. My check (29 Oct 2026): with default options, Oxlint 1.89.0 and ESLint 10.13.0 both report `isOdd` in `const isEven = (n) => (n === 0 ? true : isOdd(n - 1));` followed by `const isOdd = ...`, code that runs correctly. Written as declarations, ESLint reports the same reference plus two `func-style` errors, so under both rules top-level mutual recursion has no clean spelling.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/horwat-2008-10-007807-wayback.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/jshopl-corrected-2021-3.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/tc39-notes/meetings_2012-09_sept-20.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/nubd-oxlint.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/nubd-eslint.txt`

### 1.7 The 2012 fight over TDZ cost: Microsoft measured a slowdown, TC39 kept the TDZ. VERIFIED (es-discuss via Wayback; TC39 notes); Vendor (Microsoft) for the measurements

- **Citation:** Luke Hoban (Microsoft), "Performance concern with let/const," es-discuss, 16 Sep 2012, https://mail.mozilla.org/pipermail/es-discuss/2012-September/024993.html (Wayback 14 Dec 2014); TC39 meeting notes, 19 and 20 Sep 2012, tc39/notes `meetings/2012-09/sept-19.md` and `sept-20.md` at `53a59ad7`.
- **What it says:**
  - Hoban took V8's early-boyer benchmark, changed `var` to `let`, and "saw a consistent ~27% slowdown." With intra-procedural check removal in a Chakra prototype, "we still see an ~5% slowdown." The hard case is "closure captured let references".
  - Hoban's case against: "The value of temporal dead zones is to catch a class of programmer errors." It is "far from the most common error that lint-like tools catch today". Hoban asked to remove the TDZ so `let`/`const` would read as `undefined` before initialisation.
  - At the 19 Sep meeting, Eich relayed that "the whole JSC team is against temporal dead zones on let," and would "support it on const". Mark Miller "Presented personal experiences where dead zones were essential in detecting problems." Resolution: "No change to temporal dead zone (yet)".
  - On 20 Sep, when Dave Herman argued JS reads top to bottom, Miller answered "But it's not because you can forward-call functions,".
- **Caveat:** 2012 numbers from a prototype and one benchmark; engines changed a lot since (6.13–6.15). The notes are rough minutes.
- **For the post:** the TDZ's runtime cost and its bug-catching value were argued from the start, with no data on the bugs side. The same trade-off reappears in 2023 (6.13).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/hoban-2012-09-024993-wayback.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/tc39-notes/meetings_2012-09_sept-19.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/tc39-notes/meetings_2012-09_sept-20.md`

### 1.8 Arrow functions, from strawman to consensus (2011–2012): one arrow, lexical `this`, no generators, not constructors. VERIFIED (wiki.ecmascript.org via Wayback; Ecma TC39 minutes)

- **Citation:** Brendan Eich, "strawman:arrow_function_syntax," wiki.ecmascript.org (Wayback 9 May 2011), http://wiki.ecmascript.org/doku.php?id=strawman:arrow_function_syntax ; "harmony:arrow_function_syntax" (Wayback 7 Jun 2012; page last modified 26 May 2012), http://wiki.ecmascript.org/doku.php?id=harmony:arrow_function_syntax ; Ecma TC39, _Minutes of the 27th meeting_, 28–29 Mar 2012, Ecma/TC39/2012/020 Rev. 2. https://www.ecma-international.org/archive/ecmascript/2012/TC39/tc39-2012-020-Rev2.pdf
- **What it says:**
  - The 2011 strawman had both `->` (dynamic `this`) and `=>` (lexical `this`), modelled on CoffeeScript ("Hard to beat C# and CoffeeScript here"). It also proposed declaration forms like `const colder(c) -> {...}`, noting "Name binding forms hoist to body (var) or block (let, const) top".
  - March 2012 minutes, consensus: "Have only one arrow, namely =>", "this is always static. No provision for dynamic this." and "Other than the treatment of this, the function behaves like a normal function." Horwat's objection list included that `-> {}` "intended to return an empty object, instead returns undefined".
  - Harmony page (Eich's rationale, 29 Mar 2012): "dynamic this binding is an oft-fired footgun." "Lexical this matches the dominant cohort measured by Kevin Smith". "Arrows cannot be generators and we do not want deep continuations." "From lexical this it follows that arrow functions are not constructors". Deferred: "Optional leading Identifier for named arrow function expressions."
- **Caveat:** the wiki is gone; these are Wayback captures. Kevin Smith's measurements (the "BTF Measurements thread") were not opened.
- **For the post:** arrows were designed as expressions. The declaration forms in the strawman were dropped, and a name was deferred and never added, so `const name = () => {}` relying on name inference (1.12) is what replaced them.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/wiki-strawman-arrow-20110509.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/wiki-harmony-arrow-20120607.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/pdf/tc39-2012-020-Rev2.txt`

### 1.9 Today's spec: function declarations are initialised before the body runs; `var` is `undefined`; `let`/`const` are created but not initialised. VERIFIED (ECMA-262 2027 draft)

- **Citation:** ECMA-262, https://tc39.es/ecma262/ , FunctionDeclarationInstantiation (§10.2.11) and GlobalDeclarationInstantiation (§16.1.7).
- **What it says:**
  - FunctionDeclarationInstantiation note: "Formal parameters and functions are initialized as part of FunctionDeclarationInstantiation." "All other bindings are initialized during evaluation of the function body."
  - Each `var` name gets `CreateMutableBinding` then `InitializeBinding(name, undefined)`. For lexical declarations: "Lexically declared names are only instantiated here but not initialized." Then every function declaration is instantiated with `InstantiateFunctionObject` and stored.
  - Duplicates: "If there are multiple function declarations for the same name," "the last declaration is used."
  - GlobalDeclarationInstantiation does the same for scripts, creating global function bindings with `CreateGlobalFunctionBinding` before any statement runs.
  - The declaration itself does nothing when reached: FunctionDeclaration evaluation is `Return empty.`
- **For the post:** the whole difference between the styles at runtime is one step of these algorithms: a declaration's binding is filled in before line 1; a `const` binding stays empty until its line runs.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt` (lines ~18251–18465, ~34330–34455)

### 1.10 Today's spec: the temporal dead zone. VERIFIED (ECMA-262 2027 draft); cross-ref `import-cycles.md` 2.3

- **Citation:** ECMA-262 draft, §14.3.1 Let, Const, Using, and Await Using Declarations; §9.1.1.1.6 GetBindingValue (Declarative Environment Records).
- **What it says:** the variables "are created when their containing Environment Record is instantiated but" "may not be accessed in any way until the variable's LexicalBinding is evaluated." GetBindingValue: "If the binding exists but is uninitialized a ReferenceError is thrown," "regardless of the value of strict."
- **Caveat:** `import-cycles.md` 2.3 already quotes the first sentence; this entry adds GetBindingValue. The term "temporal dead zone" itself does not appear in the normative text I searched; it comes from the TC39 discussion (1.6).
- **For the post:** "before initialization" is about time, not text order. A `const` function used inside another function is fine as long as the call happens after the `const` line has run.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt`

### 1.11 Block-level functions and Annex B web-legacy semantics. VERIFIED (ECMA-262 2027 draft)

- **Citation:** ECMA-262 draft, BlockDeclarationInstantiation (§14.2.3); Annex B.3.2 "Block-Level Function Declarations Web Legacy Compatibility Semantics"; B.3.3 "FunctionDeclarations in IfStatement Statement Clauses"; §17.1 Forbidden Extensions.
- **What it says:**
  - On entering a block, bindings "for each block scoped variable, constant, function, or class" "declared in the block are instantiated". Functions are created and initialised there immediately (`InstantiateFunctionObject`, then `InitializeBinding`), so inside a block a function declaration is hoisted to the top of the block while `let`/`const` are not.
  - B.3.2: before ES2015 "the ECMAScript specification did not define the occurrence of a FunctionDeclaration" "as an element of a Block statement's StatementList." Browsers allowed it, but "the semantics of such declarations differ among those implementations." The spec now defines only the intersection, as "Normative Optional" steps marked inline in FunctionDeclarationInstantiation, BlockDeclarationInstantiation and GlobalDeclarationInstantiation: in sloppy code a block function also gets a function-level `var` binding, `undefined` until the block runs.
  - B.3.3: `if (x) function f() {}` is allowed, but "This production only applies when parsing non-strict code."
  - "When processing strict mode code, the extensions defined in" "B.3.1, B.3.2, B.3.3, and B.3.5 must not be supported."
- **Caveat:** the current draft folds the B.3.2 algorithm steps into the main clauses as "Normative Optional" blocks; older editions put them in Annex B. Section numbers differ across editions.
- **For the post:** in modules and classes (always strict, 1.14) block-level function declarations are simply block-scoped and hoisted within the block. The messy legacy behaviour applies only to sloppy scripts.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt` (lines ~27348–27392, ~67976–68084)

### 1.12 Name inference: `const f = () => {}` gets `f.name === "f"`, by spec. VERIFIED (ECMA-262 2027 draft) plus my check

- **Citation:** ECMA-262 draft, IsAnonymousFunctionDefinition (§8.4.3), NamedEvaluation (§8.4.5), LexicalBinding evaluation (under §14.3.1), SetFunctionName (§10.2.9), Function instances' `name` (§20.2.4.2).
- **What it says:**
  - IsAnonymousFunctionDefinition: "It determines if its argument is a function definition" "that does not bind a name."
  - LexicalBinding with an initializer: if the initializer is an anonymous function definition, the value is computed with `NamedEvaluation of Initializer with argument name`, where name is the binding identifier. InstantiateArrowFunctionExpression then performs `SetFunctionName(closure, name)`. SetFunctionName: "It adds a 'name' property to func."
  - The same rule applies to assignment to an identifier, default values in destructuring, and property definitions in object literals (the other IsAnonymousFunctionDefinition call sites).
  - On `name`: "The name has no semantic significance but is typically a variable or property name". "Anonymous function objects that do not have a contextual name associated with them" "use the empty String as the value of the 'name' property."
  - My check (Node 24.14.1/V8 13.6 and Bun 1.4.4/JSC, 29 Oct 2026): `name` is `"constArrow"`, `"constFnExpr"`, `"prop"` (object literal) and `"destructured"` (destructuring default), but `""` for `holder.assigned = function () {}` (member assignment is not an IdentifierRef) and for an arrow passed straight to a call.
- **For the post:** since ES2015, a `const` arrow has the same `name` as a declaration would. The anonymous-function argument against `func-style: expression` mostly doesn't apply to top-level `const` functions; it does apply to member assignment and inline callbacks (see 6.17 for stack traces).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/names-and-traces-node24.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/names-and-traces-bun.txt`

### 1.13 What arrows lack, in the spec's words. VERIFIED (ECMA-262 2027 draft) plus my check

- **Citation:** ECMA-262 draft, §15.3 Arrow Function Definitions; Table of function object slots (`[[ThisMode]]`); FunctionDeclarationInstantiation; MakeConstructor (§10.2.5); §20.2.4.3 `prototype`; §15.2.4–15.2.6.
- **What it says:**
  - §15.3.4 note: "An ArrowFunction does not define local bindings for arguments, super, this, or new.target." References to them "must resolve to a binding in a lexically enclosing environment."
  - Arrows are created with `lexical-this`; `[[ThisMode]]` "lexical means that this refers to the this value of a lexically enclosing function."
  - FunctionDeclarationInstantiation: "Arrow functions never have an arguments object."
  - Constructors: function declarations and expressions call `MakeConstructor(closure)` ("It converts func into a constructor."), which sets `[[Construct]]` and creates `prototype`. InstantiateArrowFunctionExpression does not. §20.2.4.3 note: functions created by bind, methods, "or an ArrowFunction do not have a 'prototype' property." Note 2 under §15.2.6: a `prototype` "is automatically created for every function defined using a FunctionDeclaration or FunctionExpression,".
  - No generator arrows: the `ArrowFunction` grammar has no `*` form, its body is `FunctionBody[~Yield, ~Await]`, and "It is a Syntax Error if ArrowParameters Contains YieldExpression is true." (Async arrows exist as `AsyncArrowFunction`.)
  - My check: `new (() => {})` throws `TypeError: A is not a constructor` in V8 and `function is not a constructor` in JSC; an arrow's `prototype` is `undefined`.
- **For the post:** an arrow is a function that can't be `new`ed, can't be a generator, and can't have its own `this` or `arguments`. For top-level helpers none of that matters; for methods and generators, `func-style` must allow a declaration or method.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/early-call-node-bun.txt`

### 1.14 Expressions vs declarations, and strict mode. VERIFIED (ECMA-262 2027 draft)

- **Citation:** ECMA-262 draft, §11.2.2 Strict Mode Code; §15.2.5 InstantiateOrdinaryFunctionExpression note; Annex C.
- **What it says:**
  - "Module code is always strict mode code." "All parts of a ClassDeclaration or a ClassExpression are strict mode code."
  - A named function expression's name is bound in its own scope: it "can be referenced from inside the FunctionExpression's FunctionBody" for recursion, but "unlike in a FunctionDeclaration, the BindingIdentifier in a FunctionExpression" "cannot be referenced from and does not affect the scope enclosing the FunctionExpression."
  - In strict code, "A this value of either undefined or null" "is not converted to the global object" (Annex C), so an unbound method call gives `this === undefined` for both declarations and expressions. Only arrows ignore the call's `this`.
  - The block-function legacy rules (1.11) are off in strict code.
- **For the post:** in an ES module (which is what most TypeScript projects compile from) the declaration/expression difference reduces to hoisting and `this`/`arguments`/constructability. The sloppy-mode traps that once justified always using expressions mostly don't apply.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt`

### 1.15 Modules: functions are ready at link time, `const` is not, and calling early fails differently per style. VERIFIED (ECMA-262 draft) plus my check; cross-ref `import-cycles.md` 2.3–2.4

- **Citation:** ECMA-262 draft, §16.2.1.7.3.1 InitializeEnvironment (Source Text Module Records).
- **What it says:**
  - InitializeEnvironment walks the module's lexically scoped declarations; for a function, generator or async function declaration it runs `InstantiateFunctionObject` and initialises the binding at link time. `const`/`let`/`class` bindings are created but left uninitialised; `var` gets `undefined`. (The cycle consequences are in `import-cycles.md` 2.3; the V8, Firefox and Safari messages are in 2.4.)
  - My check, calling each form before its line (Node 24.14.1 and Bun 1.4.4, 29 Oct 2026): a declaration runs; `var f = function () {}` gives `TypeError: f is not a function`; `let`/`const` arrows give `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'f' before initialization` in both engines. A `const` arrow that calls a later `const` works if the call happens after both lines run, and throws if it happens between them.
- **Caveat:** my check used `new Function(...)` script bodies, not modules; the TDZ behaviour is the same. Bun's JSC message matched V8's wording plus a period, which differs from the Safari wording MDN gives (`import-cycles.md` 2.4); I didn't check Safari itself.
- **For the post:** across a module cycle, `export function` survives where `export const f = () => {}` throws. That is the mechanism behind 6.1.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/ecma262-draft.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/early-call-node-bun.txt`

## 2. TypeScript

Items 2.1–2.11 are about types: what you can annotate on a `const` and not on a function declaration (2.1–2.4), and the TypeScript features that care which form you use (overloads 2.5, assertion functions 2.6, type predicates 2.7, generic arrows in `.tsx` 2.8, `this` parameters 2.9, `--isolatedDeclarations` 2.10, React component types 2.11). Items 2.12–2.14 are TypeScript's own use-before-declaration checks and a local experiment on what `tsc` catches, including the TanStack Router pattern. Items 2.15–2.20 are typescript-eslint: `no-use-before-define` (options, history, a lint run), the shared configs and related rules, and what its maintainers and ESLint's said about `func-style` in TypeScript. 2.21 is the TypeScript repo's own style. TypeScript docs are from microsoft/TypeScript-Website at `205122bb` (28 Oct 2026); compiler files from microsoft/TypeScript at tag `v6.0.3`; typescript-eslint at `v8.72.0`. Experiments ran on 29 Oct 2026 with the worktree's `tsc` 6.0.3 and `tsgo` 7.0.0-dev.20260707.2, ESLint 10.13.0, typescript-eslint 8.72.0, Oxlint 1.89.0 and Node 24.14.1; the files, raw outputs and a results table are in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/` (`RESULTS.txt`). Everything else is in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/` and `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/`.

### 2.1 The handbook's ways to type a function all describe the value, not the declaration. VERIFIED (handbook at `205122bb`); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** TypeScript Handbook, "More on Functions," microsoft/TypeScript-Website `packages/documentation/copy/en/handbook-v2/More on Functions.md` at `205122bb73dcbf6f3b373de5bdb0d9fa5434d015`. https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/2/functions.html
- **What it says:** "The simplest way to describe a function is with a _function type expression_." Such a type can be named: "we can use a type alias to name a function type". A callable with properties needs "a _call signature_ in an object type". Every example applies the type to a parameter or a variable (`const f1: voidFunc = () => {...}`), never to a function declaration, because there is no syntax for that (2.4). The handbook also flags a looseness of typing through the variable: "Contextual typing with a return type of `void`" / "does **not** force functions to **not** return something." But "when a literal function definition has a `void` return type," / "that function must **not** return anything."
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/b11-void-return.ts`):** `const cb: Callback = () => 42` with `type Callback = () => void` compiles; `function cbDecl(): void { return 42 }` fails with TS2322.
- **Caveat:** the `void` rule is deliberate (it lets `forEach(el => dst.push(el))` compile), not a bug. It still means a function typed through its variable can be checked less strictly than one annotated on its own signature.
- **For the post:** typing through the variable is real and documented, but it types the value. It's not the same as annotating the function: the `void` case shows the annotation on the variable can be looser.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/handbook-v2-More_on_Functions.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.2 Contextual typing: annotate the variable and the parameters come free. VERIFIED (handbook and reference at `205122bb`, local run); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** TypeScript Handbook, "Everyday Types" (section on anonymous functions), and Reference, "Type Inference" (section "Contextual Typing"), microsoft/TypeScript-Website at `205122bb`. https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/type-inference.html#contextual-typing
- **What it says:** "This process is called _contextual typing_" because the context "informs what type it should have." In the reference: "Contextual typing occurs when the type of an expression is implied by its location." The common cases include "right hand sides of assignments" and "arguments to function calls". Without a context, "the function's argument would implicitly have type `any`" (an error under `noImplicitAny`).
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/b10-declaration-typed-after.ts`):** `function handle(event) {...}` followed by `handle satisfies Handler;` fails with TS7006 (`Parameter 'event' implicitly has an 'any' type.`). A declaration's parameters are never contextually typed by a later check. `const viaAnnotation: Handler = (event) => event.x` compiles with `event` typed.
- **Caveat:** contextual typing applies to function expressions and arrows alike; it's about the `const`, not the arrow. A `const f: Handler = function (event) {...}` gets the same parameter types.
- **For the post:** this is the precise version of claim (2), that a function is easier to type through its variable: with a `const`, one annotation on the name types every parameter and the return; with a declaration, each parameter and the return must be written out.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/handbook-v2-Everyday_Types.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/reference-Type_Inference.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.3 `satisfies` (TypeScript 4.9) works on function expressions, not declarations. VERIFIED (release notes at `205122bb`, local run); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** TypeScript 4.9 release notes, "The `satisfies` Operator," microsoft/TypeScript-Website `release-notes/TypeScript 4.9.md` at `205122bb`. https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/release-notes/typescript-4-9.html
- **What it says:** `satisfies` "lets us validate that the type of an expression matches some type," "without changing the resulting type of that expression." The notes' examples are object literals; functions aren't mentioned.
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/b9-satisfies-function.ts`):** `const viaSatisfies = ((event) => event.x) satisfies Handler` contextually types `event` and keeps the narrower return type: calling it gives `number`, while the annotated `viaAnnotation: Handler` gives `unknown` (TS18046 on `a + 1`). There is no `function f() {} satisfies T` form; the open request is 2.4.
- **Caveat:** `satisfies` needs an expression, so it needs a `const` (or parentheses around a function expression). It's a 4.9 feature; older code bases can't use it.
- **For the post:** `satisfies` sharpens claim (2): a `const` can be checked against a type and still keep its own inferred type, which a declaration can't do at all.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/release-notes-TypeScript_4.9.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.4 Seven years of requests to type a function declaration; one declined, the rest open or folded in. VERIFIED (issue JSON, comments and events); Vendor (Microsoft) for team statements

- **Citation:** microsoft/TypeScript issues #16918 (Jessidhia, 4 Jul 2017, open, Suggestion + In Discussion, 20 thumbs-up), #22063 (mbrowne, 20 Feb 2018, open, Suggestion + In Discussion + Has Repro, 125 thumbs-up, 41 comments), #24942 (dotproto, 14 Jun 2018, labelled Declined by mhegazy, closed by bot 28 Jun 2018), #34319 (fabiancook, 14 Oct 2019, open, Suggestion + In Discussion, 93 thumbs-up), #40378 (Jack-Works, 4 Sep 2020, Awaiting More Feedback, closed by its author as not planned 14 Apr 2025), #41411 (RebeccaStevens, 5 Nov 2020, closed the same day in favour of #40378), #51556 (dummdidumm, 16 Nov 2022, open, Suggestion + Awaiting More Feedback, 352 thumbs-up, 28 comments); experimental PR #57859 (a-tarasyuk, 20 Mar 2024, closed unmerged by its author 24 Mar 2025). https://github.com/microsoft/TypeScript/issues/22063
- **What it says:**
  - #22063 uses React as the case: `const Test: React.FC<TestProps>` works, but a declaration "can't, at least not directly". The author prefers declarations, yet "TypeScript is basically forcing the use of expressions, due to the issues described above." They note "the current idiom is to just prefer arrow functions for cases like this".
  - #16918: "making it a function declaration prevents it from being typed as `React.SFC`." Without it, every parameter type must be written and the return type isn't checked at the definition.
  - #24942, the one team decision on record (mhegazy, 14 Jun 2018): "Also `implements` does not contextually type the declaration," and "i think the use of an interface and a function expression is superior pattern" "both in terms of value (contextual types) and readability (syntax)." DanielRosenwasser on the same thread: "it's frustrating that declaring a decorator requires using different function syntax".
  - #51556: lacking `satisfies` on declarations "forces a certain coding style where people have to use arrow functions" "when they rather want to use functions." RyanCavanaugh labelled it Awaiting More Feedback on 17 Nov 2022; no team comment since. Of PR #57859 its author wrote: "there's no final consensus on certain cases."
- **Caveat:** labels and the 2018 Declined label are the only team positions; no MEMBER-association comment on #22063, #16918 or #51556 states a view (RyanCavanaugh's one comment on #34319 is about pinging). Thumbs-up counts are at 29 Oct 2026. mhegazy's association now shows as CONTRIBUTOR; in 2018 they applied the Declined label.
- **For the post:** the strongest support for claim (2) comes from people who prefer declarations: they say TypeScript pushes them to `const` when a function must match a type. The team's only stated view (2018) is that the expression plus a type is the better pattern.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ts-issue-22063.json`, `ts-issue-22063-comments.json`, `ts-issue-22063-events.json`, `ts-issue-16918*.json`, `ts-issue-24942*.json`, `ts-issue-34319*.json`, `ts-issue-40378*.json`, `ts-issue-41411*.json`, `ts-issue-51556*.json`, `ts-pr-57859*.json` (all under `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/`)

### 2.5 Overloads need a declaration; a `const` needs a call-signature type and an `any` implementation. VERIFIED (handbook, local run, ESLint source and PR); Vendor (Microsoft) for the handbook

- **Citation:** Handbook "More on Functions," section "Function Overloads," at `205122bb`; ESLint PR #19755, "feat: ignore overloaded function declarations in func-style rule" (sethamus, opened 23 May 2025, merged 27 May 2025), and `lib/rules/func-style.js` and `docs/src/rules/func-style.md` at `v10.13.0`; typescript-eslint issue #1236 (kachkaev, 19 Nov 2019, closed 29 Sep 2025). https://github.com/eslint/eslint/pull/19755
- **What it says:** overloads are written as "_overload signatures_" "followed by the body of the function". "The signature of the _implementation_ is not visible from the outside." The handbook's advice: "Always prefer parameters with union types instead of overloads when possible". ESLint now exempts them from `func-style`: "Overloaded function declarations are not reported as errors by this rule." In #1236 (a team on `func-style` "expression"), a reply showed that a `const` typed with two call signatures rejects an implementation typed `(x: string | number) => string | number`.
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/b6-overloads.ts`, `b7-overload-then-const.ts`):** a declaration with two overloads compiles. `const parseConst: Parse = (input: any): any => ...` compiles only because of `any`; with `string | number` it fails with TS2322. Overload signatures followed by a `const` fail with TS2391 ("Function implementation is missing or not immediately following the declaration.") and TS2300. ESLint 10.13.0 and Oxlint 1.89.0 `func-style` ("expression") don't report the overloaded declaration (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/lint/eslint-func-style.out`, `oxlint-func-style.out`).
- **Caveat:** the exemption is recent in ESLint (May 2025). The `const` form type-checks the implementation against the overload set only loosely, because it needs `any`.
- **For the post:** a concrete cost of forcing expressions, now acknowledged by both linters: overloads can't be written as a `const` without giving up checking of the implementation.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/handbook-v2-More_on_Functions.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/eslint/docs-func-style@v10.13.0.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/eslint/func-style@v10.13.0.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/eslint-pr-19755.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/tseslint-issue-1236*.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.6 Assertion functions (TypeScript 3.7) work as declarations, or as a `const` only with an annotation on the variable (TS2775). VERIFIED (PRs #32695 and #33622, diagnosticMessages at `v6.0.3`, issues, local run); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** Anders Hejlsberg, "Assertions in control flow analysis," microsoft/TypeScript PR #32695 (opened 3 Aug 2019, merged 23 Sep 2019); Hejlsberg, "Error when assertion function calls aren't CFA'd," PR #33622 (26–30 Sep 2019); `src/compiler/diagnosticMessages.json` at `v6.0.3`; issues #34523 (mweststrate, 16 Oct 2019, labelled Design Limitation by RyanCavanaugh, 35 thumbs-up) and #45385 (devanshj, 9 Aug 2021, open, Awaiting More Feedback); TypeScript 3.7 release notes at `205122bb`. https://github.com/microsoft/TypeScript/pull/32695
- **What it says:** TypeScript 3.7 "introduces a new concept called 'assertion signatures'". The PR's rule: a call is analysed as an assertion only if "each identifier in the function name references an entity with an explicit type". "An entity is considered to have an explicit type when it is declared" / "as a function, method, class or namespace, or as a variable, parameter" / "or property with an explicit type annotation." The reason: "This particular rule exists so that control flow analysis" / "of potential assertion calls doesn't circularly trigger further analysis." RyanCavanaugh in #45385: "This rule exists so that the feature could be added without drastically impacting performance." The diagnostics (both Error): TS2775 "Assertions require every name in the call target" / "to be declared with an explicit type annotation." and TS2776 "Assertions require the call target to be an identifier or qualified name." On #34523, jcalz: "This is (perhaps unfortunately) working as intended".
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/b1`–`b4`):** `const assertIsString = (value: unknown): asserts value is string => {...}` with no annotation on the variable: the call fails with TS2775 and `input` stays `unknown` (TS18046). Typed through the variable (`const assertIsString: AssertIsString = (value) => {...}`) and as a declaration, both narrow. A method on an object literal with an inferred type also gets TS2775.
- **Caveat:** the 3.7 release notes don't mention this requirement; their examples are all declarations. The PR is where it's stated.
- **For the post:** a case where the two styles really differ in TypeScript: a declaration is an assertion function as written; an arrow needs its whole type repeated on the variable. It cuts both ways for claim (2): here the `const` needs more annotation, not less.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ts-pr-32695.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ts-pr-33622.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/typescript/diagnosticMessages@v6.0.3.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ts-issue-34523*.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ts-issue-45385*.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/release-notes-TypeScript_3.7.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.7 Type predicates work on arrows with no annotation on the variable. VERIFIED (release notes at `205122bb`, local run); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** TypeScript 5.5 release notes, "Inferred Type Predicates" (section written by Dan Vanderkam, who implemented it in PR #57465), at `205122bb`. https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/release-notes/typescript-5-5.html
- **What it says:** the notes' examples include arrows, such as `const isNumber = (x: unknown) => typeof x === 'number';` inferred as `x is number`. "Previously, TypeScript would have just inferred that these functions return `boolean`."
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/b5-type-predicate-arrow.ts`):** an arrow with a written `value is string` predicate narrows at the call site with no variable annotation, and `.filter((v) => typeof v === "string")` yields `string[]`.
- **Caveat:** predicates (`x is T`) and assertions (`asserts x is T`) look alike but follow different rules; only assertions need the explicit-type rule in 2.6.
- **For the post:** keeps 2.6 in proportion: the arrow/declaration difference is specific to assertion signatures.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/release-notes-TypeScript_5.5.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.8 Generic arrows in `.tsx` need `<T,>`; a design limitation since 2017. VERIFIED (issues, release notes, local run); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** microsoft/TypeScript #15713, "Generic usage reported as JSX Error" (jrwebdev, 10 May 2017, labelled Design Limitation, closed 17 May 2017); #47062 (giladgo, 8 Dec 2021, Bug, closed); TypeScript 5.5 release notes at `205122bb`. https://github.com/microsoft/TypeScript/issues/15713
- **What it says:** mhegazy: "This is a limitation cased by the use of `<T>`" (sic), "combined with JSX grammar, it makes such components ambiguous to parse." Their workaround: "The workaround is to use a function expression instead". In #47062, RyanCavanaugh: "We should lookahead one token here for `=`" / "the same way we do for `,`". The 5.5 release notes write `const isNonNullish = <T,>(x: T) => x != null;` in a `tsx` block.
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsx-parse/c1-generic-arrow.tsx`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/c2-generic-arrow-comma.tsx`):** `<T>(value: T): T => value` in `.tsx` fails to parse (TS17008, TS1382, TS1005); `<T,>`, `<T extends unknown>` and a generic declaration all compile.
- **Caveat:** a syntax wart, not a type-system difference; formatters may rewrite `<T,>`. It affects only arrows (function expressions and declarations are fine).
- **For the post:** a small, real cost of arrows-only in React code: every generic component or hook written as an arrow needs the trailing comma.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ts-issue-15713*.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ts-issue-47062*.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/release-notes-TypeScript_5.5.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsx-parse/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.9 A `this` parameter is for declarations and function expressions; an arrow can't declare one (TS2730). VERIFIED (handbook, diagnosticMessages, local run); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** Handbook "More on Functions," section "Declaring `this` in a Function," at `205122bb`; `diagnosticMessages.json` at `v6.0.3`. https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/2/functions.html#declaring-this-in-a-function
- **What it says:** TypeScript uses the `this` parameter slot "to let you declare the type for `this` in the function body." "Note that you need to use `function` and not arrow functions" "to get this behavior". The diagnostic: TS2730 "An arrow function cannot have a 'this' parameter."
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/b8-this-parameter.ts`):** declaration and `const ... = function (this: Button)` compile; the arrow fails with TS2730.
- **Caveat:** Ultracite's `func-style` setting still allows function expressions, so `const f = function (this: T) {}` is a way out; whether other rules (such as `prefer-arrow-callback` or `func-names`) object depends on the setup.
- **For the post:** callback-style APIs that set `this` (DOM handlers, some plugin APIs) need a `function`, so arrows-only has exceptions by design.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/handbook-v2-More_on_Functions.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/typescript/diagnosticMessages@v6.0.3.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/types/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.10 Under `--isolatedDeclarations`, a typed `const` satisfies the rule with one annotation. VERIFIED (release notes, local run); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** TypeScript 5.5 release notes, "Isolated Declarations," at `205122bb`. https://www.typescriptlang.org/docs/handbook/release-notes/typescript-5-5.html#isolated-declarations
- **What it says:** the option "reports errors when a module can't be reliably transformed without a type-checker." The notes' example error is a declaration with no return type.
- **Local check (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/isolated/d1-isolated-declarations.ts`):** an exported declaration without a return type and a bare exported arrow both fail with TS9007 ("Function must have an explicit return type annotation with --isolatedDeclarations."). A declaration with a full signature passes, and so does `export const arrowTyped: Handler = (event) => event.x`.
- **Caveat:** opt-in, and only for exported functions. Both styles can satisfy it; the typed `const` does it with one annotation when a named function type already exists.
- **For the post:** another concrete instance of claim (2), limited to code that publishes types.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ts-website/release-notes-TypeScript_5.5.md@205122bb.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/isolated/tsc-6.0.3.out`

### 2.11 React components: `React.FC` is the textbook case of typing through the variable, and the community moved away from it. VERIFIED (PR, `@types/react` files, cheatsheet at a pinned commit); arguments ANECDOTAL; Vendor (Meta) for the template

- **Citation:** Retsam, "Remove React.FC from Typescript template," facebook/create-react-app PR #8177 (opened 13 Dec 2019, merged by ianschmitz 22 Jan 2020); `@types/react` `index.d.ts` at 17.0.80, 18.0.0 and 19.3.0 (unpkg and the worktree's installed copy); typescript-cheatsheets/react `docs/basic/getting-started/function-components.md` at `c550e41b98f7ec20d993eb3c2acc4cbf6eac2e29` (9 Sep 2026). https://github.com/facebook/create-react-app/pull/8177
- **What it says:** the PR: "`React.FC` is unnecessary: it provides next to no benefits and has a few downsides." The first downside: "Defining a component with `React.FC` causes it to implicitly take `children`". Also: "Doesn't support generics." Its reason for touching the template: "the prominence of this template makes it a de facto source of 'best practice'". The cheatsheet now says "the general consensus today is that `React.FunctionComponent`" / "(or the shorthand `React.FC`) is not needed." and, in a code comment, "With latest React types and TypeScript 5.1. it's mostly a stylistic choice, otherwise discouraged." Its own examples use `const App = ({ message }: AppProps) => ...`, annotating the parameter, not the variable. In `@types/react` 17.0.80 the call signature takes `PropsWithChildren<P>`; from 18.0.0 it takes `P`; 19.3.0 has `(props: P): ReactNode | Promise<ReactNode>`.
- **Caveat:** the PR's main argument (implicit `children`) stopped being true with `@types/react` 18; the generics argument still holds. A single contributor's write-up, merged by one maintainer.
- **For the post:** the most common real use of typing a function through its variable has fallen out of favour; current React advice types the props parameter, which works the same on a declaration. That weakens claim (2) for React code specifically.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/cra-pr-8177.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/cra-pr-8177-comments.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/types-react/index.d.ts@17.0.80.ts`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/types-react/index.d.ts@18.0.0.ts`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/types-react/index.d.ts@19.3.0-lines1030-1100.ts`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/react-ts-cheatsheet/function-components@c550e41b.md`

### 2.12 TypeScript's use-before-declaration errors and the rule behind them: only uses that run immediately, in the same file. VERIFIED (diagnosticMessages and `checker.ts` at `v6.0.3`); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** microsoft/TypeScript `src/compiler/diagnosticMessages.json` and `src/compiler/checker.ts` (`isBlockScopedNameDeclaredBeforeUse`, `checkResolvedBlockScopedVariable`) at tag `v6.0.3`. https://github.com/microsoft/TypeScript/blob/v6.0.3/src/compiler/checker.ts
- **What it says:** the messages (all category Error): TS2448 "Block-scoped variable '{0}' used before its declaration."; TS2449 "Class '{0}' used before its declaration."; TS2450 "Enum '{0}' used before its declaration."; TS2454 "Variable '{0}' is used before being assigned." (TS2454 is definite-assignment analysis, not ordering.) In the checker, a use in another file passes because "nodes are in different files and order cannot be determined". A later declaration "can be legal if usage is deferred" "(i.e. inside function or in initializer of instance property)". The "deferred" test treats any enclosing function as deferred unless it's an immediately invoked function expression (`getImmediatelyInvokedFunctionExpression`).
- **Caveat:** "deferred" is syntactic: the checker doesn't ask whether the enclosing function is called before the declaration runs. That's why the cases in 2.13 slip through.
- **For the post:** `tsc` catches a use that runs while the module is still initialising only when the use is written directly at that level (or in an IIFE). Anything inside a function body, or in another module, is assumed safe.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/typescript/diagnosticMessages@v6.0.3.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/typescript/compiler/checker@v6.0.3.ts`

### 2.13 What `tsc` catches: 4 of 10 temporal dead zone crashes in a local test. VERIFIED (local run: tsc 6.0.3, tsgo 7.0.0-dev.20260707.2, Node 24.14.1)

- **Citation:** hand-written files `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/tdz/a1`–`a11`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/cycle/*`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/tanstack/*`, run 29 Oct 2026; table in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/RESULTS.txt`.
- **Method:** 20 small cases. Each was type-checked with `tsc --strict` (and `tsgo`, which gave the same diagnostics), then `tsc`'s emitted JavaScript was run with Node to see which throw.
- **What it shows:** 10 cases throw `ReferenceError: Cannot access '…' before initialization`. `tsc` reports TS2448/TS2449 on 4 of them: a `const` arrow called at top level before its declaration (a1), an IIFE reading a later `const` (a4), a class used before its declaration (a9), and the TanStack route with `component: Page` above `const Page` (t1). It misses 6: a function declared first that reads a later `const` and is called at top level (a2); a synchronous `.map` callback reading a later `const` (a5); a hoisted function declaration called first that reads a later `const` (a7); a route options helper called before `Page` exists (t4); and two import cycles where one module reads the other's `const` at top level (t5, `cycle-const`). Uses inside function bodies that run later (a3, t2, t6, `cycle-deferred`) neither error nor crash. The same import cycle with a function declaration (`cycle-decl`) runs, because the declaration is initialised when the module is instantiated.
- **Caveat:** small hand-made cases, chosen to probe the checker's rule, not a sample of real code; they show what can be missed, not how often. TS2454 also fires on a8 (`let` read before assignment) and a11 (`var`), which don't throw.
- **For the post:** the evidence for "`tsc` still catches real temporal dead zone crashes": it catches the direct case, not crashes reached through a call or across modules. Two cases (a7, a miss, and `cycle-decl`, which runs) also show the other side: hoisting a declaration doesn't hoist the `const` it reads, while in a cycle the declaration is what saves you.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/RESULTS.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/tdz/tsc-6.0.3.out`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/tdz/node-runtime.out`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/cycle/tsc-6.0.3.out`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/cycle/node-runtime.out`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/tanstack/tsc-6.0.3.out`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/tanstack/node-runtime.out`

### 2.14 The TanStack Router exemption: true for the default options, and the TS2448 backstop covers only the direct case. VERIFIED (Ultracite source and history, local run)

- **Citation:** Ultracite `packages/cli/config/oxlint/tanstack/index.mjs` and `eslint/tanstack/eslint.config.mjs` at `7bf1b7be`; commit `c5be8480` (Hayden Bleasel, 5 Sep 2026, "Resolves #789"); haydenbleasel/ultracite issue #789 (413n, 29 Aug 2026, closed 6 Sep 2026); local files `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/tanstack/` with a stub `createFileRoute` (not the real library).
- **What it says:** the preset comment: "No declaration order satisfies the rule, and `tsc` still catches" / "real temporal dead zone crashes (TS2448)." The issue reported "174 findings across the route trees of four apps", using a function declaration for the component, and argued "Function hoisting makes the code correct at runtime either way".
- **What the local run shows (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/RESULTS.txt`):**
  - t1, `export const Route = createFileRoute(...)({ component: Page })` above `const Page`: crashes; `tsc` TS2448. The claim holds here.
  - t2, `const Page` above `Route`: safe; flagged by `no-use-before-define` defaults (`Route` inside `Page`), not by `{ variables: false }`.
  - t3, `Route` above `function Page`: safe; flagged by defaults, not by `{ functions: false }`. This is the shape the issue reported.
  - t4, an options helper `const routeOptions = () => ({ component: Page })` called in `createFileRoute(...)(routeOptions())` above `Page`: crashes; `tsc` silent; `no-use-before-define` defaults catch it.
  - t5, the component in another file that imports `Route` back and reads `Route.id` at top level: crashes; `tsc` and every lint setting silent. t6, same cycle reading `Route` only when rendering: safe.
- **Caveat:** stub, not TanStack; the real router's code splitting may change module order. t4 is contrived. "No declaration order satisfies the rule" is true for the rule's defaults with a `const` component; with `{ functions: false }` and a function declaration (blocked by `func-style` and `react/function-component-definition` in Ultracite), t3 satisfies it, and with `{ variables: false }` t2 does.
- **For the post:** the exemption's reasoning is sound for the common case, and the honest version is narrower: `tsc` catches the route-above-`const` crash, not crashes reached through a call or an import cycle. The conflict exists because `func-style` forbids the hoisted component the router docs' pattern relies on (as reported in #789).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ultracite/oxlint-tanstack-index.mjs`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ultracite/eslint-tanstack-eslint.config.mjs`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/src/ultracite/commit-c5be8480.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/text/ultracite-issue-789.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/RESULTS.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/lint/eslint-tseslint-defaults.out`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/lint/eslint-tseslint-variables-false.out`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/B/check/lint/eslint-tseslint-functions-false.out`

### 2.15 typescript-eslint `no-use-before-define` at v8.72.0: what the options and defaults do. VERIFIED (docs and source at `v8.72.0`, issue #10389)
