### 3.27 Which TDZ cases each tool catches. My check (local run)

- **Method:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/C/check/ts/tdz.ts` with three cases: (1) `export const early = tooEarly();` above `const tooEarly`; (2) a `const` arrow whose body calls a `const` declared below it; (3) `const a = run(() => a + 1)` where `run` calls its callback immediately (ESLint #20014). `tsc` 6.0.3 `--strict`, the three linters as in 3.26, and Node 24.14.1 for case 3.
- **Result:** case 1 crashes; `tsc` reports TS2448 ("Block-scoped variable 'tooEarly' used before its declaration.") and all three linters report it. Case 2 is safe; ESLint and Oxlint report it, `tsc` and Biome do not. Case 3 throws `ReferenceError: Cannot access 'a' before initialization` in Node; no linter and not `tsc` reports it (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/C/check/out-tsc-tdz.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/C/check/out-tdz-three-linters.txt`).
- **For the post:** in TypeScript files, the crash that `no-use-before-define` caught was one the compiler already reports; its extra reports were safe orderings; and a real crash through a callback passed every tool. Three cases are an illustration, not a rate.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/C/check/ts/tdz.ts`, `out-tsc-tdz.txt`, `out-tdz-three-linters.txt`, `runtime/case3.mjs`

## 4. What configs, style guides and other languages choose

What the widely used JavaScript and TypeScript configs and style guides set for `func-style`, `no-use-before-define` and the rules around them, the reasons they give, and how other languages handle definition order. 4.1 is the who-sets-what table; 4.2–4.4 Airbnb (guide, config, and the issue threads); 4.5–4.7 Google; 4.8 StandardJS; 4.9 XO and Unicorn; 4.10 eslint-config-love; 4.11 eslint-config-prettier; 4.12 antfu; 4.13 @vercel/style-guide; 4.14 Deno; 4.15 Node.js core; 4.16 ESLint's and webpack's own configs; 4.17 the React and Next.js docs; 4.18–4.20 Crockford (Code Conventions, JSLint, _The Good Parts_); 4.21–4.26 other languages (Python, Go, Rust, C, OCaml and Haskell, and a summary). Config files are in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/`, style guides in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/google/`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/deno/`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/jslint/`, other languages in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/`, and issue and PR JSON in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/`. Everything was fetched on 29 Oct 2026 at the SHAs given.

### 4.1 Who sets what: function style in popular configs (Oct 2026). VERIFIED (config source at pinned SHAs)

- **Citation:** each file read at the SHA in the table. eslint-plugin-react 7.37.5 (installed) for the default of `react/function-component-definition`: `namedComponents` falls back to `'function-declaration'` (`lib/rules/function-component-definition.js`).

| Config (version, SHA) | `func-style` | `no-use-before-define` | `prefer-arrow-callback` | `arrow-body-style` | `func-names` | Component or top-level rule |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ultracite, Oxlint core (for comparison) | **error, `expression`, `allowArrowFunctions`** (ESLint preset the same) | **error, defaults** (functions, classes, variables all checked) | error, defaults | error, `as-needed` | error (`always`) | `react/function-component-definition` `namedComponents: "arrow-function"`; `unicorn/consistent-function-scoping` error |
| eslint-config-airbnb-base 15.0.0 (`8ed19247`) | `['off', 'expression']`, comment "TODO: enable" | error, all three `true` | error, `allowNamedFunctions: false`, `allowUnboundThis: true` | error, `as-needed` | warn | eslint-config-airbnb 19.0.4: `namedComponents: ['function-declaration', 'function-expression']` |
| eslint-config-standard 17.1.0 (`7d01bbc3`); neostandard 0.14.1 (`2d12a3e6`) | not set | error, `functions`, `classes`, `variables` all `false` | not set | not set | not set | none |
| eslint-config-xo 7.0.0 (`f9950756`) | not set | not set | error, `allowNamedFunctions: true` | error (default `as-needed`) | error, `never` | `unicorn/consistent-function-scoping` off |
| eslint-config-google 0.14.0 (`0273839f`, archived) | commented out | commented out | commented out | commented out | commented out | none |
| gts 7.0.0 (`bd623c03`) | not set | `@typescript-eslint/no-use-before-define` off | error | not set | not set | none |
| eslint-config-love 158.0.0 (`fc9a5b5f`) | listed as intentionally unused | `@typescript-eslint/` version, error, all five options `false` | error, `allowNamedFunctions: true`, `allowUnboundThis: true` | error, `as-needed` | listed as intentionally unused | none |
| typescript-eslint 8.72.0 `recommended`, `strict`, `stylistic` | not set | not set | not set | not set | not set | `all` only: `@typescript-eslint/no-use-before-define` error (core rule off) |
| eslint-plugin-unicorn 79.0.0 `recommended` (`d078ad9e`) | `unicorn/consistent-function-style` off | n/a | n/a | `unicorn/consistent-arrow-return-style` off | n/a | `unicorn/consistent-function-scoping` error |
| eslint-config-prettier 10.1.8 (`9b0b0a47`) | n/a | n/a | not turned off by the main config; off in `eslint-config-prettier/prettier` | same | n/a | n/a |
| @vercel/style-guide 6.0.0 (`1377fa3b`, archived) | not set | not set | not set | not set | error, `as-needed` | `react/function-component-definition` warn, default options (function declarations) |
| @antfu/eslint-config 10.0.0 (`54bfe6ac`) | not set | error, `functions: false`, `classes: false`, `variables: true` (and `ts/` version) | error, `allowNamedFunctions: false`, `allowUnboundThis: true` | not set | not set | `antfu/top-level-function` error (off with `lessOpinionated`) |
| Node.js core `eslint.config.mjs` (`8d514c7b`) | **error, `declaration`, `allowArrowFunctions`** | error, `classes: true`, `functions: false`, `variables: false` | not set | not set | not set | none |
| eslint-config-eslint 14.0.0, ESLint's own codebase (`4637f982`, tag v10.13.0) | **error, `declaration`** | error, defaults | error | error, `as-needed` | not set | none |
| eslint-config-webpack 4.15.0 (`470f52ae`) | **error, `declaration`, `allowArrowFunctions`, `allowTypeAnnotation`** | error, all `true` (TS: also `enums`, `typedefs`) | error, `allowNamedFunctions: false`, `allowUnboundThis: true` | error, `as-needed` | error, `as-needed` | none |
| Deno style guide (`denoland/docs` `74beb758`); deno_lint 0.84.1 (`e9ae6b77`) | guide: top-level functions use `function` (4.14) | no such rule in deno_lint | no such rule | no such rule | no such rule | none of the 121 deno_lint rules covers function style |

- **Reading it:** of the configs that set `func-style` at all, the four that enforce it (Node.js core, ESLint's own, eslint-config-webpack, and antfu's equivalent rule) all require declarations. The only `expression` setting is Airbnb's, and it has been `off` since it was written (4.2). Where `no-use-before-define` is on, function declarations are usually exempt: Standard, neostandard, love, antfu and Node.js set `functions: false`; Airbnb, ESLint's own config and eslint-config-webpack check them.
- **Caveat:** these are configs, not usage data. Airbnb's npm 15.0.0 was published from an earlier commit; the rule files at HEAD still say 15.0.0. typescript-eslint's configs don't touch core rules except to swap extension rules. _Not set_ means the config leaves the rule at ESLint's default, off; `@eslint/js` 10.0.1 `recommended` turns on none of these rules.
- **For the post:** Ultracite's pairing (expressions required, functions checked by `no-use-before-define`) appears in none of the configs above. The configs that enforce a style chose declarations, and most configs that order-check variables exempt function declarations because they're hoisted.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/` (airbnb in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/`), `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/eslint-plugin-react/function-component-definition.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/node/eslint.config.mjs`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/eslint-config-webpack-javascript.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/eslint-config-webpack-typescript.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/deno/`

### 4.2 Airbnb's 7.1 flipped from declarations to named expressions on 7 Sep 2016, and the config has never enforced it. VERIFIED (README at tags and HEAD, commit, rule files)

- **Citation:** airbnb/javascript `README.md` at `8ed19247` (HEAD, 21 Feb 2026), at tag `eslint-config-airbnb-v10.0.0` (`9ffadff1`, 1 Aug 2016) and `eslint-config-airbnb-v11.0.0` (`f769e350`, 8 Sep 2016); commit `ccd18f8b40e1718b5e75d6702ecbc2ab82d28455` (Jordan Harband, 7 Sep 2016); `packages/eslint-config-airbnb-base/rules/style.js`, `es6.js`, `variables.js` at `8ed19247`.
- **What it says:**
  - Until Sep 2016, 7.1 read "Use function declarations instead of function expressions." Its reason: "Function declarations are named, so they're easier to identify in call stacks." and the hoisted body, plus "This rule makes it possible to always use" arrows "in place of function expressions."
  - Commit `ccd18f8b` ("[guide] Update declarations section to prefer expressions.", "Fixes #1008.") reversed it. Today: "Use named function expressions instead of function declarations." Why: "Function declarations are hoisted, which means that it’s easy - too easy -" "to reference the function before it is defined in the file." "This harms readability and maintainability." If a definition gets in the way, "perhaps it’s time to extract it to its own module!" And "Don’t forget to explicitly name the expression," because "This eliminates any assumptions made about the Error’s call stack." The good example is `const short = function longUniqueMoreDescriptiveLexicalFoo() {`; an anonymous `const foo = function () {` and a declaration are both "bad".
  - The same commit changed the config line to `'func-style': ['off', 'expression'],` under the comment "TODO: enable". It is unchanged at HEAD, ten years later.
  - The other function rules are on: `prefer-arrow-callback` (8.1: "When you must use an anonymous function" "(as when passing an inline callback), use arrow function notation."; why: "which is usually what you want, and is a more concise syntax."), `arrow-body-style` `as-needed` (8.2: "Syntactic sugar. It reads well when multiple functions are chained together."), and `no-use-before-define` with all options (14.5: "Variables, classes, and functions should be defined before they can be used."; otherwise "it can harm readability since a reader won't know" "what a thing that's referenced is.").
  - Issue #1813 (2018) noted the guide's own "good" examples still use declarations. Harband: "when we switched from 'use declarations' to 'use expressions'," "we failed to clean up all the examples."
- **Caveat:** the 2016 reason for declarations (names in stack traces) was overtaken by ES2015 name inference, which is what #794 argued (4.3); the new reason is about order, which `no-use-before-define` already covers.
- **For the post:** the best-known guide that says "use expressions" chose them in 2016 for a reason (avoid relying on hoisting) that its own lint config enforces another way, and it never turned `func-style` on.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/README.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/README-v10.0.0.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/README-v11.0.0.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/packages_eslint-config-airbnb-base_rules_style.js`, `..._es6.js`, `..._variables.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/airbnb-commit-ccd18f8b40.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/airbnb-issue-1813.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/airbnb-issue-1008.json`

### 4.3 Airbnb's maintainer: arrows are for inline callbacks, components are never arrows, and names must be explicit (2016–2026). VERIFIED (issues); ANECDOTAL (one maintainer's position)

- **Citation:** airbnb/javascript #794 (opened 21 Mar 2016, closed 26 Aug 2016), #767 (1 Mar 2016), #1541 (2017), #2505 (11 Nov 2021) and PR #2501 (merged 22 Dec 2021), #2829 (2023), #3121 (opened 25 Apr 2025, open). Answers by Jordan Harband (`ljharb`, collaborator). Airbnb's React guide, `react/README.md` at `8ed19247`.
- **What it says:**
  - On name inference (#794, 2016): "Function name inference is implemented in hardly any of the browsers we support." and "relying on function name inference hinders greppability". In 2017: "It remains preferred to use only explicit names, and never rely on inference." and "Explicit > implicit :-)". On #767: "it's useful to be able to grep for function names".
  - On where arrows belong (#3121, 2025–2026): "Basically any time a function isn’t an inline callback," "it should be a traditional function." "arrow functions are best confined to inline callbacks only." "This guide strictly forbids relying on hoisting," and "Nobody using this guide should ever be relying on hoisting."
  - On components (#2505): eslint-config-airbnb 19.0.0 shipped `namedComponents` allowing only function expressions, which the reporter called "This seems like a configuration mistake,"; #2501 added `function-declaration`. When users asked for arrows: "Components should never be arrow functions, so this change is by design." Reasons: "arrow functions can never have explicit names, only implicit ones via ES6 name inference," and wrapping `export const Foo = (props) => {}` in `React.memo` means "your 'Foo' name suddenly vanishes, because you were relying on inference,". And "arrow functions are conceptually meant to replace inline callbacks." "They are _not_ a general-purpose function replacement," "An arrow function doesn't properly convey the importance/noun-ness of a react component." The React guide's example: "prefer normal functions (not arrow functions) over classes", with an arrow component marked bad "(relying on function name inference is discouraged)".
  - On `prefer-arrow-callback` banning named callbacks (#1541, 2017): "It's set to the default value; there's no intention behind it."
- **Caveat:** these are one maintainer's answers. The memo example is a real effect of inference (the name comes from the variable, not the inner function), but I didn't measure how often it matters in DevTools or stack traces.
- **For the post:** the maintainer of the guide most associated with "use expressions" wants arrows only for callbacks and treats arrow components as wrong, the opposite of Ultracite's `namedComponents: "arrow-function"`. The memo example is a concrete case where the arrow style loses a name.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/airbnb-issue-794.json`, `airbnb-issue-767.json`, `airbnb-issue-1541.json`, `airbnb-issue-2505.json`, `airbnb-issue-2501.json`, `airbnb-issue-2829.json`, `airbnb-issue-3121.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/react_README.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/airbnb/packages_eslint-config-airbnb_rules_react.js`

### 4.4 Airbnb on hoisting and order: `no-use-before-define` is the override people ask for, and the answer is modules (2015–2022). VERIFIED (issues); ANECDOTAL

- **Citation:** airbnb/javascript #273 (2 Apr 2015), #1089 "What rules do the community tend to override?" (opened 25 Sep 2016, open, 151 comments; reaction counts via GraphQL on 29 Oct 2026), #1861 (9 Jul 2018), #2657 "Newspaper metaphor support" (17 Oct 2022).
- **What it says:**
  - #273 asked for a hoisting rule because "function hoisting is the only thing in JavaScript which breaks that rhythm." A reply: "With this rule you have to read the code backwards:".
  - #1089's opening post lists `no-use-before-define`: "some enjoy using hoisting to define helper methods at the bottom of the file." "This guide discourages relying on hoisting;". The first reply (jongleberry, 66 thumbs-up) lists "`no-use-before-define` - disallows function declarations at the bottom". Chad Watson (27 thumbs-up): "I personally don't like to scroll down to the bottom of a file" "to see what a module really does." Another override was for "two mutually recursive functions that referenced each other." In 2022 a user pointed to the votes for `nofunc`; Harband: "nope, because this project doesn’t make decisions based on voting." "Functions always belong declared before they’re used."
  - #1861 (Clean Code's top-down order): "Relying on hoisting makes code harder to maintain," and "`no-use-before-define` is critical in ensuring that."
  - #2657 asked for the "newspaper metaphor" (high-level code first). Harband: "The 'newspaper metaphor' is not a good way to make readable code" "in a world of modules". "If something's not important to read first, move it to another module" "instead of relying on hoisting." Then: "function declarations with the `no-use-before-define` eslint rule also avoid relying on hoisting," and "it's just that using declarations _without_ the lint rule risks relying on hoisting." Closing: "The point of style guides is to enforce largely subjective rules" "in an opinionated fashion." "If it were objective, no guide would be needed."
- **Caveat:** reaction counts are a self-selected signal from one thread, not a survey. The reporter on #2657 (`rkrisztian`) cites Clean Code; I didn't open the book.
- **For the post:** the maintainer concedes the key point: declarations plus `no-use-before-define` already prevent relying on hoisting. What Airbnb's rule buys over that is a single style, not order. The top-down reading order users ask for is exactly what `no-use-before-define` forbids with either style.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/airbnb-issue-273.json`, `airbnb-issue-1089.json`, `airbnb-issue-1861.json`, `airbnb-issue-2657.json`, `airbnb-search.txt`

### 4.5 Google's TypeScript guide: "Prefer function declarations for named functions", and `const foo = () => 42;` is the bad example. VERIFIED; Vendor (Google)

- **Citation:** google/styleguide `tsguide.md` at `5d6fa5e1d2d33a7b080a6aef4c8e0ee5a9b358aa` (29 Oct 2026), section "Functions". Published at https://google.github.io/styleguide/tsguide.html#function-declarations
- **What it says:** "Prefer function declarations over arrow functions or function expressions when" "defining named functions." Good: `function foo() { return 42; }`; bad: `const foo = () => 42;`. The exception: "Arrow functions _may_ be used, for example, when an explicit type annotation is required." (example: `const fooSearch: SearchFunction = (source, subString) => { ... };`). Nested functions may use either; "In method bodies in particular," "arrow functions are preferred because they have access to the outer `this`." Then "Do not use function expressions. Use arrow functions instead." (exceptions: rebinding `this`, generators). On bodies: "Top level functions use function declarations." and "Only use a concise body if the return value of the function" "is actually used." The guide marks `myPromise.then((v) => console.log(v));` as bad for that reason.
- **Caveat:** no reason is given for preferring declarations. The concise-body rule is about leaked return values, which `arrow-body-style: as-needed` doesn't consider (it would accept the "bad" `then` callback).
- **For the post:** the guide gives one exception: a function typed through its variable. That's the maintainer's reason (2), and Google's answer is to allow the arrow there, not require it everywhere. Deno's maintainers point to this rule as precedent (7.7).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/google/tsguide.md`

### 4.6 Google's JavaScript guide: top-level arrows in the examples since Aug 2019, after declarations in 2017. VERIFIED (guide at three commits); Vendor (Google)

- **Citation:** google/styleguide `jsguide.md` at `5d6fa5e1` (2026), `jsguide.html` at `dceb47fb3ea99ad7cc4308fa2c9ecb0d012639e1` ("Update JS styleguide (#464)", 27 Aug 2019) and at `c212f99186958f25d3d20994144ea11df923da79` (8 May 2017); PR #464.
- **What it says:** §5.5.1 today: "Top-level functions may be defined directly on the `exports` object, or else" "declared locally and optionally exported." Both examples are arrows (`exports.processString = (str) => {` and `const processString = (str) => {`). §5.5.2: "If it is useful to give the function a name," "it should be assigned to a local `const`." §5.5.3: "Arrow functions provide a concise function syntax and simplify scoping `this`" "for nested functions." "Prefer arrow functions over the `function` keyword for" nested functions, and "The single expression form is preferred if it improves readability". In May 2017 the same section's examples were declarations (`function helperFunction()` and two more), plus one `exports.foo` arrow. The switch landed in the Aug 2019 import (it appeared and was reverted once on 30 Jan 2018). PR #464's description is only "See issue #462".
- **Caveat:** I found no stated reason for the 2019 change. The JS guide targets Closure's `goog.module` code, the TS guide (4.5) TypeScript; both are current.
- **For the post:** Google's two current guides disagree: the JS guide shows top-level `const` arrows, the TS guide calls the same line bad. Both styles are defensible enough that one company's guides picked both.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/google/jsguide.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/google/jsguide-dceb47fb.html`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/google/jsguide-c212f991.html`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/google-jsguide-commits-2017-2019.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/google-styleguide-pr-464.json`

### 4.7 Google's lint configs set almost none of these rules. VERIFIED (source); Vendor (Google)

- **Citation:** google/eslint-config-google `index.js` 0.14.0 at `0273839f` (repository archived); google/gts `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/index.js` 7.0.0 at `bd623c03`.
- **What it says:** eslint-config-google lists `func-style`, `func-names`, `no-use-before-define`, `arrow-body-style` and `prefer-arrow-callback` only as commented-out `off` lines. gts turns on `prefer-arrow-callback` and turns `@typescript-eslint/no-use-before-define` off; it sets no function-style rule.
- **Caveat:** gts layers `eslint-config-prettier` and then enables `prefer-arrow-callback` while also running `prettier/prettier`, the combination 4.11 warns about.
- **For the post:** Google's published guides take positions; its published lint configs don't enforce them.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/google/eslint-config-google-index.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/gts/src-index.js`

### 4.8 StandardJS: `no-use-before-define` with functions, classes and variables all allowed, and no function-style rule (2016–2017). VERIFIED (config, issues); reasons ANECDOTAL

- **Citation:** standard/eslint-config-standard `.eslintrc.json` v17.1.0 (`7d01bbc3`); neostandard `lib/configs/base.js` v0.14.1 (`2d12a3e6`); standard/standard #381 (11 Jan 2016), #636 (24 Sep 2016), #659 (12 Oct 2016), #1144 (31 May 2018, open); eslint-config-standard PR #59 (28 Nov 2016).
- **What it says:** the rule is `["error", { "functions": false, "classes": false, "variables": false }]` in both. Feross Aboukhadijeh on #636: "We currently do not enable the `no-use-before-define` rule" "because it triggers way too many false positives." The plan: "using function declarations before they're defined is allowed (hoisting)", "using classes before they're defined is **not** allowed", and a variable used inside a function is allowed because "This is allowed since it _is_ possible to use `printFoo()` correctly", "And lots of users of `standard` do this." Daniel Cousens on mutual recursion with the rule's defaults: "No valid ordering exists with the rule as it stands AFAIK." and "it makes recursive or circular functions completely impossible otherwise." Aboukhadijeh (Jan 2017): "We don't want users to think that pleasing `standard` is a lot of work." On a function-style rule (#381), Cousens: "I personally opt for `function save`," "but I don't think there is a hard-rule for this in standard." v17.1.0 sets none of `func-style`, `prefer-arrow-callback` or `arrow-body-style`; #1144 (asking for `arrow-body-style`) is still open.
- **Caveat:** the shipped config allows classes too, unlike the 2016 plan. I didn't trace the commit that added the rule.
- **For the post:** the zero-config JavaScript preset closest to Ultracite in spirit turns `no-use-before-define` on only for clearly wrong same-scope uses, so that hoisted functions and calls from inside functions stay legal. Cousens' mutual-recursion point applies to Ultracite's setting, where `const` functions get no exemption (see the notes for Hayden at the end of this file).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/standard/eslintrc-v17.1.0.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/neostandard/base.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/standard-issue-381.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/standard-issue-636.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/standard-issue-659.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/standard-issue-1144.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/standard-config-pr-59.json`

### 4.9 XO and Unicorn: Sorhus calls hoisting "a misfeature", but neither enforces a function style, and Unicorn's 2026 rule reports nothing by default. VERIFIED (source, docs, issue, PR)

- **Citation:** sindresorhus/eslint-plugin-unicorn #3 "`func-style` like rule" (James Talmage, opened 14 Apr 2016, closed 12 Jun 2026); PR #3137 "Add `consistent-function-style` rule" (Sindre Sorhus, merged 12 Jun 2026; released in v66.0.0, 14 Jun 2026); `rules/consistent-function-style.js`, `docs/rules/consistent-function-style.md`, `consistent-function-scoping.md`, `consistent-arrow-return-style.md` at v79.0.0 (`d078ad9e`); xojs/eslint-config-xo `source/javascript-rules.js`, `source/plugins-rules.js` v7.0.0 (`f9950756`).
- **What it says:**
  - #3, 2016. Talmage: "My personal preference is to prefer `declaration` style, as it allows hoisting", but ESLint's rule has no exception for reassigned functions, "so it is impractical to enforce." Jeroen Engels: "(and I don't like hoisting btw)" and "So I'm rather against enforcing it." Sorhus: "I prefer arrow functions for everything as they're lexically scoped (no dumb implicit `this`)" "and you can use `const` with them to ensure they're never overridden." "I consider hoisting a misfeature. I prefer explicitness." But: "I don't feel super strongly either way, just slightly prefer arrow functions." and later "I agree that ES2015 exports should use a normal function."
  - PR #3137 ("Fixes #3") closed the issue ten years later with `consistent-function-style`, `recommended: false`. Its doc: "By default, this rule reports nothing. Configure the roles you want to enforce." It won't autofix: "Function declarations, function expressions, arrow functions, and object methods are not always semantic equivalents," "so `--fix` would be too risky for a style rule." Its roles include separate `reassignedVariables` and `typedVariables`, which allow only expressions or arrows.
  - Unicorn's `recommended` turns on `consistent-function-scoping` ("Move function definitions to the highest possible scope."); `consistent-arrow-return-style` and `prefer-short-arrow-method` are off.
  - XO 7.0.0 sets `func-names` to `never`, `prefer-arrow-callback` with `allowNamedFunctions: true`, `arrow-body-style` on, no `func-style`, and turns `unicorn/consistent-function-scoping` off with "TODO: Remove this override when the rule is more stable."
- **Caveat:** the "misfeature" view is one author's 2016 comment; the configs that author maintains never enforced it.
- **For the post:** the most prolific lint-plugin author shares the maintainer's dislike of hoisting and still left function style unenforced for a decade, and the rule that finally shipped refuses to autofix because the forms aren't equivalent. That's direct support for treating conversions as risky.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/unicorn-issue-3.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/unicorn-pr-3137.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/unicorn/` (all), `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/xo/javascript-rules.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/xo/plugins-rules.js`

### 4.11 eslint-config-prettier: `arrow-body-style` and `prefer-arrow-callback` are "special rules" because their autofix can produce invalid code. VERIFIED (source, README, changelog)

- **Citation:** prettier/eslint-config-prettier v10.1.8 (`9b0b0a47`): `README.md` ("Special rules"), `prettier.js`, `index.js`, `CHANGELOG.md`; prettier/eslint-plugin-prettier v5.5.7 (`e6dd27a4`): `README.md`, `recommended.js`.
- **What it says:** the rules "might cause problems if using [eslint-plugin-prettier] and `--fix`." eslint-plugin-prettier's README: "you can in some cases end up with invalid code" "due to a bug in ESLint’s autofix", "where you manually have to insert a missing closing parenthesis". Since 7.0.0 (5 Dec 2020) they "are no longer turned off by default."; only `eslint-config-prettier/prettier` (pulled in by `plugin:prettier/recommended`) sets both to 0. Marked special in 4.0.0 (26 Jan 2019).
- **Caveat:** this applies only when Prettier runs inside ESLint through eslint-plugin-prettier. Ultracite runs the formatter as a separate step, which the README says is safe.
- **For the post:** a small, documented case where the arrow-conversion fixes and a formatter interact badly. Worth a sentence, not a section.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/eslint-config-prettier/README.md`, `prettier.js`, `index.js`, `CHANGELOG.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/eslint-plugin-prettier/README.md`, `recommended.js`

### 4.12 antfu's config: `antfu/top-level-function` turns top-level arrows into declarations, except typed ones and one-liners. VERIFIED (source, docs, issue); reason ANECDOTAL

- **Citation:** antfu/eslint-plugin-antfu v3.3.1 (`dd881422`): `src/rules/top-level-function.ts`, `.md`; antfu/eslint-config v10.0.0 (`54bfe6ac`): `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/stylistic.ts`, `javascript.ts`, `typescript.ts`; the rule was added in commit `87d26fb6` ("feat: enforce to use function declaration on top-level", 29 Mar 2023); antfu/eslint-config #178 (22 Apr 2023).
- **What it says:** "Enforce top-level function to be declared using `function`" "instead of arrow function or function expression." The message: "Top-level functions should be declared with function keyword". It autofixes. It skips a variable with a type annotation ("When the variable is assigned with types, it rule will ignore it.") and, in the source but not the docs, a concise arrow whose body ends on the declaration's line. On #178 a user wrote "I think using hoisting to break the function declare order" "is not a good habit." Anthony Fu: "It's my **opinionated** config." and later "For consistency. And `function` keyword is more semantically readable/distinguishable." "I consider top-level arrow function is a bad practice." The same config sets `no-use-before-define` with `functions: false`.
- **Caveat:** one author's preference, stated as such.
- **For the post:** a popular config enforces the exact opposite of Ultracite with an autofix, and its two exemptions (typed variables, one-line arrows) are the two cases where the maintainer's reasons (1) and (2) are strongest.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/antfu-plugin/top-level-function.md`, `top-level-function.ts`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/antfu/stylistic.ts`, `javascript.ts`, `typescript.ts`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/antfu-config-issue-178.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/antfu-search.txt`

With no options, eslint-plugin-react 7.37.5 requires function declarations for named components (4.1). `func-names` is `['error', 'as-needed']`. No `func-style`, `no-use-before-define` or `prefer-arrow-callback`.

### 4.14 Deno's style guide: "Top-level functions should not use arrow syntax", with a reason added only in May 2025. VERIFIED (guide, PRs, issue)

- **Citation:** denoland/docs `runtime/contributing/style_guide.md` at `74beb758`; denoland/docs issue #222 (7 Dec 2023, closed 6 May 2025), PRs #1685 (merged 6 May 2025) and #1690 (merged 8 May 2025); deno_lint 0.84.1 rule list at `e9ae6b77`.
- **What it says:** "Top-level functions should use the `function` keyword. Arrow syntax should be" "limited to closures." The bad example is `export const foo = (): string => {`. The reason: "Regular functions and arrow functions have different behavior with respect to" "hoisting, binding, arguments, and constructability." "The `function` keyword" "clearly indicates the intent to define a function, improving legibility and" "traceability while debugging." Issue #222: "Provide rationale for prohibiting arrow syntax on top-level". #1685 removed the section instead; Bartek Iwańczuk: "this section just induced confusion instead of being useful." Yoshiya Hinosawa: "I think this is just a preference of the team." and "google typescript style guide has a similar rule". David Sherret: "the reason to use function declarations over variable declarations with an arrow function" "is clarity, consistency, and hoisting." #1690 restored it: "We still prefer and are comfortable with this rule." deno_lint has no rule for it (121 rules, none on function style or use-before-define).
- **Caveat:** the guide covers Deno's own code, not users'.
- **For the post:** a runtime team wrote the rule first and the reason two years later, and one maintainer called it a team preference. The written reason cites hoisting as a point in favour of declarations, the opposite of the maintainer's reason (3).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/deno/style_guide.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/deno/deno_lint_rules_list.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-docs-issue-222.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-docs-pr-1685.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-docs-pr-1690.json`

### 4.15 Node.js core: `func-style: declaration` since Apr 2017, and `no-use-before-define` with functions exempt. VERIFIED (config, commits, PRs)

- **Citation:** nodejs/node `eslint.config.mjs` at `8d514c7b` (29 Oct 2026); commit `aea7269c45f91fd983e8ca91fddd4021fc40d099` (Rich Trott, 28 Apr 2017), PR #12711; PR #14032 (1 Jul 2017); PR #27366 (23 Apr 2019, closed unmerged).
- **What it says:** today `'func-style': ['error', 'declaration', { allowArrowFunctions: true }],` and `no-use-before-define` with `classes: true`, `functions: false`, `variables: false`. The 2017 commit: "Except for arrow functions, require function declarations instead of" "function expressions via linting." The PR text gives the motive: nits to contributors "to avoid potential confusion over the Temporal Dead Zone" (7.4). The `no-use-before-define` options come from #14032 (7.5).
- **For the post:** a large codebase chose declarations-with-arrows-allowed, the mirror image of Ultracite's `expression` setting, and gave the TDZ as a reason.

### 4.16 ESLint's own codebase and webpack: declarations, with `no-use-before-define` fully on. VERIFIED (source)

- **Citation:** eslint/eslint `packages/eslint-config-eslint/base.js` 14.0.0 at tag v10.13.0 (`4637f982`); webpack/eslint-config-webpack `configs/javascript.js`, `configs/typescript.js` 4.15.0 (`470f52ae`); webpack/webpack `eslint.config.mjs` at `b31e011e`.
- **What it says:** ESLint's config sets `"func-style": ["error", "declaration"]` (no arrow exception), `"no-use-before-define": "error"` (defaults), `"prefer-arrow-callback": "error"` and `"arrow-body-style": ["error", "as-needed"]`. eslint-config-webpack sets `func-style` `declaration` with `allowArrowFunctions` and `allowTypeAnnotation`, and `no-use-before-define` with everything checked. webpack's own repo turns `no-use-before-define` off with the comment "Revisit it in future", and turns `func-style` and `func-names` off for its ES5 runtime files (`lib/**/*.runtime.js`, `hot/*.js`).
- **Caveat:** no reasons recorded in either file.
- **For the post:** ESLint's maintainers are the one team that pairs a strict order rule with a strict style rule, and they pick declarations: every function is written `function f() {}` and defined above its first use. That shows the maintainer's ordering goal (3) doesn't require expressions. webpack's config uses ESLint's `allowTypeAnnotation`, the typed-variable exception again (4.5, 4.12).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/configs/eslint-config-eslint/base.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/eslint-config-webpack-javascript.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/eslint-config-webpack-typescript.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/webpack-eslint.config.mjs`

### 4.17 The React and Next.js docs write every component as a function declaration, and name the function inside `memo`. VERIFIED (docs source; my count); Vendor (Meta, Vercel)

- **Citation:** reactjs/react.dev at `0821e7d9` (27 Oct 2026): `src/content/learn/*.md` (52 files), `src/content/reference/react/memo.md`; vercel/next.js at `fcd3fdeb` (30 Oct 2026 UTC): `docs/01-app/01-getting-started/*.mdx` (19 files).
- **Method:** grep on 29 Oct 2026 for lines that start a PascalCase function declaration versus `const PascalCase = (...) =>`. Counts lines in code samples, so repeated examples count more than once.
- **What it says:** react.dev learn pages: 1,051 declaration lines, 0 arrow-component lines. Next.js getting-started: 221 and 0. React's tutorial explains `function Profile() { }` and notes "React components are regular JavaScript functions," without discussing arrows; the arrow notes it has are about callbacks, including the block-body trap: "you _have to_ write a `return` statement yourself." "If you forget it, nothing gets returned!" The `memo` reference always passes a named function expression, as in `const Greeting = memo(function Greeting({ name }) {`. Neither site says why it uses declarations.
- **Caveat:** docs style isn't a recommendation, and neither site's lint config was checked for these rules. My grep is approximate.
- **For the post:** the two docs sites agents learn React from use declarations throughout. The `memo(function Greeting(...))` pattern is reported by `prefer-arrow-callback` at its defaults (Oxlint 1.89.0 and ESLint 10.13.0, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/check/memo-results.txt`), so code copied from React's own reference fails Ultracite as written.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/react-dev/` (learn pages), `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/react-dev-ref/memo.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/next-docs/`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/component-style-counts.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/check/memo.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/check/memo-results.txt`

### 4.18 Crockford, "Code Conventions for the JavaScript Programming Language": "All functions should be declared before they are used." VERIFIED

- **Citation:** Douglas Crockford, "Code Conventions for the JavaScript Programming Language," crockford.com, https://www.crockford.com/code.html (fetched 29 Oct 2026; undated, and its examples use `let`, so the current text postdates ES2015).
- **What it says:** under "Function Declarations": "All functions should be declared before they are used." "Inner functions" "should come after the outer function's variable declarations." "This helps make it clear" "what variables are included in its scope." The examples use `function outer(c, d) {` declarations, `let integer = function (` for a function value, and "Use of global functions should be minimized."
- **Caveat:** the page is undated. Crockford asks for declare-before-use and still writes declarations.
- **For the post:** the oldest well-known JavaScript convention already separates the two questions: functions defined before use, written as declarations.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/crockford/code.html`

### 4.19 JSLint: declare before use (2014), and arrows only with expression bodies (2020). VERIFIED (archived docs in jslint-org/jslint)

- **Citation:** jslint-org/jslint branches `website_v2014.7.8` (`lint.html`, `bbc56cfb`) and `website_v2020.11.6` (`help.html`, `e1b2dde9`). The current `index.html` on master (`78ec2dd5`, JSLint 2026.9.29) no longer carries the help text.
- **What it says:** 2014: "JSLint expects that a function will be declared before it is used." 2020: "The var statement suffers from a bad practice called hoisting." "The let statement does not do hoisting and respects block scope." A name "should be declared before it is used." The function statement "should be used in a file or function body," "but not inside of a block." "The function expression unfortunately looks like the function statement." For arrows, JSLint requires parentheses and forbids a left brace after `=>` "to avoid syntactic ambiguity." (expression bodies only).
- **Caveat:** JSLint is Crockford's tool, so this restates 4.18 and 4.20 as lint rules.
- **For the post:** JSLint enforced define-before-use for functions years before ESLint, and allowed arrows only as one-expression functions, roughly the line the TS guide (4.5) and antfu (4.12) draw.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/jslint/lint-v2014.7.8.html`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/jslint/help-v2020.11.6.html`

### 4.20 Crockford, _JavaScript: The Good Parts_ (2008), Appendix B: hoisting "leads to sloppiness". VERIFIED (publisher preview of one section, via Wayback)

- **Citation:** Douglas Crockford, _JavaScript: The Good Parts_, O'Reilly, 2008, ISBN 9780596517748, Appendix B "Bad Parts", section "The function Statement Versus the function Expression." Read: http://web.archive.org/web/20221126165548/https://www.oreilly.com/library/view/javascript-the-good/9780596517748/apbs09.html (O'Reilly's content preview).
- **What it says:** "A function statement is shorthand for a var statement with a function value." "Throughout this book, I have been using the second form" "because it makes it clear that foo is a variable containing a function value." "To use the language well, it is important to understand that functions are values." On hoisting: "This relaxes the requirement that functions should be declared before used," "which I think leads to sloppiness." "It also prohibits the use of function statements in if statements."
- **Caveat:** only this section's preview was opened, not the rest of the book. The book is pre-ES2015 (`var`, no `const` or arrows).
- **For the post:** the maintainer's reason (3) has a long pedigree: Crockford made the same argument, and preferred the `var f = function` form, in 2008. Pair it with 4.18: Crockford's later conventions keep declare-before-use and use declarations.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/goodparts/wayback-20221126165548-apbs09.html`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/goodparts/wayback-20221126165548-apbs09.txt`

### 4.21 Python: `def` is a statement that runs in order, and names in a body resolve when it's called. VERIFIED (docs); Pylint E0601 VERIFIED (source at tag)

- **Citation:** The Python Language Reference, "4. Execution model" and "8. Compound statements" (Python 3.15.2 documentation), https://docs.python.org/3/reference/executionmodel.html, https://docs.python.org/3/reference/compound_stmts.html (fetched 29 Oct 2026); pylint-dev/pylint `pylint/checkers/variables.py` and `doc/data/messages/u/used-before-assignment/` at v4.1.3 (`e75de10c`).
- **What it says:** "A function definition is an executable statement." "The function definition does not execute the function body;" "this gets executed only when the function is called." A name used before it's bound raises `NameError` or `UnboundLocalError`; "This can lead to errors when a name is used within a block" "before it is bound." "This rule is subtle." Pylint's E0601 `used-before-assignment`: "Emitted when a local variable is accessed before its assignment took place." Its example is `print(hello)` above `hello = "Hello World !"`.
- **Caveat:** that two module-level functions can call each other in either order follows from these two statements; the docs don't say it in one sentence.
- **For the post:** Python has no hoisting, like a JS `const`: a `def` exists only once its line has run, but a function body can refer to a later function because lookup happens at call time. Pylint flags the real error (use at run time before binding), not a reference inside a function body.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/python/executionmodel.html`, `compound_stmts.html`, `pylint-variables.py`, `pylint-used-before-assignment-bad.py`, `pylint-used-before-assignment-good.py`

### 4.22 Go: package-level order doesn't matter; local function values do. VERIFIED (spec)

- **Citation:** The Go Programming Language Specification, "Language version go1.28 (October 9, 2026)", `doc/go_spec.html` in golang/go at `72edc307`.
- **What it says:** "The scope of an identifier denoting a constant, type, variable, or function" "(but not method) declared at top level (outside any function) is the package block." Inside a function, "The scope of a constant or variable identifier declared inside a function begins" "at the end of the ConstSpec or VarSpec". Package variables initialize by dependency: "Dependency analysis does not rely on the actual values of the variables," "only on lexical references to them in the source, analyzed transitively."
- **Caveat:** a local `f := func() {...}` can't refer to itself; Go code declares `var f func()` first. I didn't find that pattern stated in the spec.
- **For the post:** Go makes the same split as JavaScript, enforced by the compiler: top-level `func` in any order, local function values after their declaration.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/go/go_spec.html`

### 4.23 Rust: "Items may be defined in any order"; `let` closures start after the `let`. VERIFIED (Reference)

- **Citation:** The Rust Reference, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/items.md` and `src/names/scopes.md` in rust-lang/reference at `3989fe91` (20 Oct 2026).
- **What it says:** "Items may be defined in any order, with the exception of" `macro_rules`. Name resolution "allows items to be defined before or after where the item is referred to" in the module or block. `let` statement "bindings range from just after the `let` statement until the end of the block".
- **For the post:** Rust's `fn` items behave like hoisted declarations and its `let` closures like `const` arrows.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/rust/items.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/rust/scopes.md`

### 4.24 C: declare before use, and C99 removed implicit function declarations. VERIFIED (WG14 N1256)

- **Citation:** ISO/IEC 9899:TC3 Committee Draft, WG14/N1256, 7 Sep 2007 (C99 with corrigenda), https://www.open-std.org/jtc1/sc22/wg14/www/docs/n1256.pdf ; Foreword ¶5, §6.2.1¶7, §6.5.1 footnote 79.
- **What it says:** among the "Major changes from the previous edition": "remove implicit function declaration". Scope: "Any other identifier has scope that begins just after the completion of its declarator." Footnote 79: "Thus, an undeclared identifier is a violation of the syntax."
- **Caveat:** I didn't open the C23 draft (N3220).
- **For the post:** C is the strictest case: a function must be declared (often by a prototype) before a call. C99 removed the old fallback of guessing a declaration from the call.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/c/n1256.txt` (and `n1256.pdf`)

### 4.25 OCaml needs `let rec` for recursion; Haskell's `let` is "mutually-recursive". VERIFIED (manual, report)

- **Citation:** The OCaml Manual 5.5, "The core language" and "The OCaml language: Expressions", https://ocaml.org/manual/5.5/coreexamples.html, https://ocaml.org/manual/5.5/expr.html ; Haskell 2010 Language Report, §3.12, https://www.haskell.org/onlinereport/haskell2010/haskellch3.html
- **What it says:** OCaml: "Recursive functions are defined with the let rec binding:" and "Recursive definitions of names are introduced by let rec". A plain `let` doesn't put the name in scope of its own definition; `let rec ... and ...` is how two functions refer to each other. Haskell: let expressions "introduce a nested, lexically-scoped, mutually-recursive list of declarations".
- **For the post:** the ML family makes order explicit and opt-in recursion part of the syntax; Haskell goes the other way. Neither treats the order of definitions as a style question.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/ocaml/coreexamples.html`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/ocaml/expr.html`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/other/haskell/haskellch3.html`

### 4.26 Summary: where definition order is a language rule. Synthesis of 4.21–4.25

| Language | Named top-level functions | Local function values | Who enforces it |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| JavaScript | declarations hoisted; `const` in TDZ until its line runs | same | lint (`no-use-before-define`), or a runtime `ReferenceError` |
| Python | `def` runs in order; bodies look names up at call time | same | runtime `NameError`; Pylint E0601 |
| Go | any order (package block) | after the declaration | compiler |
| Rust | any order (items) | after the `let` | compiler |
| C | declared before the call (prototype) | n/a | compiler (since C99) |
| OCaml | in order; `let rec` for recursion | same | compiler |
| Haskell | not checked (I read only §3.12, `let`) | `let` declarations are mutually recursive | compiler |

- **For the post:** most compiled languages let named functions appear in any order and require local values in order, which is the split JavaScript's declarations and `const` functions already make. Only C and OCaml require named functions in order, and both have explicit syntax (prototypes, `let rec`) for the cases where that's impossible. JavaScript has none, so a `no-use-before-define` that checks `const` functions has no answer for mutual recursion but a disable comment (see the notes for Hayden at the end of this file).

## 5. Reading order and comprehension

This section covers the two conventions for ordering functions in a file and what research says about order and reading. The conventions are callers above callees (Clean Code's stepdown rule and newspaper metaphor), which in JavaScript needs hoisting, and definitions above uses (C, Pascal, F#, Airbnb). Items 5.1–5.7 are the conventions and what their authors said. 5.8–5.15 are studies of method order, reading linearity and navigation. 5.16–5.21 are studies comparing lambdas, arrows or anonymous functions with alternatives, and 5.22 lists what no study covers. Sources are saved in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/` and `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/`. Two cross-references: `sort-keys.md` 4.8–4.12 (member order, Biegel et al., Baum et al., Ko et al.) and `cognitive-complexity.md` §3 (comprehension metrics). `cognitive-complexity.md` §3 does not cover the Brooks, Pennington or von Mayrhauser and Vans models, so 5.15 gives the minimum on them. `length-limits.md` 1.10–1.12 explains what of Clean Code can be opened, and 4.46–4.52 cover splitting and comprehension.

**One distinction to keep.** "Top-down" and "bottom-up" mean two unrelated things in this literature. In file order they mean callers first or callees first. In comprehension models they mean hypothesis-driven reading versus building meaning from statements (5.15). No study found links the two.

### 5.1 Clean Code's stepdown rule and newspaper metaphor: the headings can be verified, the text can't. VERIFIED (publisher sample pages: contents and index); book text only as quoted in 5.8 and 5.2

- **Citation:** Robert C. Martin, _Clean Code: A Handbook of Agile Software Craftsmanship_, Prentice Hall, © 2009, ISBN 978-0-13-235088-4. Pearson sample pages: https://ptgmedia.pearsoncmg.com/images/9780132350884/samplepages/9780132350884.pdf . Safari Books Online section previews via the Wayback Machine (2014 captures): `.../9780136083238/functions/ch03lev1sec3` and `.../formatting/ch05lev1sec2`.
- **What the sample shows:** Chapter 3's contents list "Reading Code from Top to Bottom: The Stepdown Rule" on p. 37, under "One Level of Abstraction per Function". Chapter 5 lists "The Newspaper Metaphor" (p. 77), "Vertical Distance" (p. 80) and "Vertical Ordering" (p. 84). The index has "dependent functions, formatting, 82–83", "source files" compared to newspaper articles, 77–78, and "The Step-down Rule, 37", with a hyphen, where the contents spell it without one.
- **The book's words, secondhand:** Geffen and Maoz (5.8) quote Chapter 5: "In general we want function call dependencies to point in the downward direction." and "a function that is called should be below a function that does the calling". That is the only verbatim text of the ordering advice found in a source that could be opened.
- **Caveat:** the sample pages hold the front matter, Chapter 1 and the index, not Chapters 3 or 5. The archived Safari previews stop after each section's first paragraph, for example "Mixing levels of abstraction within a function is always confusing." The O'Reilly chapter previews (2019 captures) stop after the chapter openings. I found no publisher sample chapter or Martin blog post with the stepdown text. The many web summaries (DZone, agent skills, Stack Overflow) are paraphrases.
- **For the post:** cite the headings and page numbers to the book, and the downward-dependency sentence through Geffen and Maoz. Don't put a summary of the stepdown rule inside quotation marks.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/pearson-clean-code-samplepages.txt`, `wayback-safari-cleancode-ch03lev1sec3.html`, `wayback-safari-cleancode-ch05lev1sec2.html`, `wayback-oreilly-cleancode-ch03.html`, `wayback-oreilly-cleancode-ch05.html`

### 5.2 Martin and Ousterhout on method order: order helps a reader, or no order fixes entanglement. VERIFIED (the joint document at a pinned commit)

- **Citation:** John Ousterhout and Robert C. Martin, johnousterhout/aposd-vs-clean-code, `README.md` at `2ce0742228fbf850e15101f83308de2cd72144b2`. https://github.com/johnousterhout/aposd-vs-clean-code (cross-reference `length-limits.md` 1.12).
- **What it says:** defending the `PrimeGenerator` split, Martin says "the functions are presented in the order they are called." and that a reader by then has seen the loop. Martin's general view: "if you trust the names of the methods being called" "then understanding the caller does not require understanding the callee". Ousterhout replies: "If methods are entangled, there is no clever ordering" "of the method definitions that will fix the problem." In the agreed summary, Ousterhout writes: "You believe that ordering the methods in a class can help to" "compensate for entanglement between methods; I don't." Martin: "I think this is a fair assessment of our agreements and disagreements."
- **Caveat:** two authors' opinions on one Java example. Java has no hoisting question, so "the order they are called" is free there. In JavaScript with `const` functions and `no-use-before-define`, it isn't.
- **For the post:** the best primary statement of both positions. Martin orders callers first to guide the reader. Ousterhout says order can't rescue code that needs reading in two places at once.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/aposd/README.md`

### 5.3 C: declared before use, defined anywhere. VERIFIED (C99 committee draft N1256)

- **Citation:** ISO/IEC 9899:TC3 Committee Draft, WG14/N1256, 7 Sep 2007. https://www.open-std.org/jtc1/sc22/wg14/www/docs/n1256.pdf
- **What it says:** the Foreword lists among C99's changes "remove implicit function declaration". §6.5.1: "An identifier is a primary expression, provided it has been declared" as an object or a function. §6.2.1p7: "Any other identifier has scope that begins just after the completion of its declarator."
- **Caveat:** C requires a **declaration** before use, not a **definition**. A prototype at the top lets the definitions appear in any order, so C allows top-down files at the cost of a prototype. In practice, putting `static` helpers above their callers avoids writing prototypes, which is where the bottom-up habit comes from. That habit is an inference; I found no C standard text or open K&R copy that prescribes file order. Before C99, a call to an undeclared function was implicitly declared. Another helper covers C more fully.
- **For the post:** define-before-use in C is a compiler constraint with an escape hatch (prototypes), not a readability claim.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/c99-n1256.txt`

### 5.4 Pascal: identifiers defined before use, `forward` for mutual recursion, and a one-pass compiler. VERIFIED (Wirth's 1973 report, OCR of the scan; unofficial HTML copy of ISO 7185)

- **Citations:** Niklaus Wirth, _The Programming Language Pascal (Revised Report)_, ETH Zürich, Berichte der Fachgruppe Computer-Wissenschaften 5, July 1973, scan hosted at http://www.standardpascal.org/The_Programming_Language_Pascal_1973.pdf . ISO 7185:1990 Pascal, unofficial HTML copy at http://www.standardpascal.org/iso7185.html
- **What it says:** Wirth's abstract: "A one-pass compiler has been constructed for the CDC 6000 computer" family. ISO 7185 §6.2.2.9: "The defining-point of an identifier or label shall precede all applied occurrences" of it, with one exception for pointer types. §6.1.4: "The only directive shall be the required directive forward". The standard's example functions `ReadExpression` and `ReadOperand` "are declared forward since they are mutually recursive".
- **Caveat:** I didn't find `forward` in the 1973 report (OCR searched; it appears in the ISO standard). The ISO copy says itself that it is "unofficial" and was made by OCR. The OCR of Wirth's scan may contain errors elsewhere; I checked the quoted line by eye against the page image (`scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/check/ocr/p-02.png`).
- **For the post:** define-before-use in Pascal came with single-pass compilation, which is the reason Geffen and Maoz give (5.8). A language that needs `forward` for mutual recursion is the clearest case of bottom-up order being a compiler constraint.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/wirth-pascal-1973.ocr.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/iso7185.txt`

### 5.5 F#: dependency order as a feature (Wlaschin, 2013). ANECDOTAL (practitioner blog)

- **Citation:** Scott Wlaschin, "Cyclic dependencies are evil", _F# for Fun and Profit_, 27 May 2013. https://fsharpforfunandprofit.com/posts/cyclic-dependencies/
- **What it says:** "One of the most common complaints about F# is that it requires" "code to be in dependency order". Wlaschin quotes "war stories from Brian McNamara" and McNamara's defence of the rule: "This is a burden you want." "There are dependencies among the classes and methods in your system." A system that forces that order is said to steer you toward "cleaner architectures, better system layering, and fewer needless dependencies."
- **Caveat:** an argument with war stories about .NET assemblies, not data on reading. The ordering is enforced across files and within them.
- **For the post:** the strongest practitioner case that bottom-up order is a feature: it makes the dependency graph visible and blocks cycles. That is the hoisting-as-crutch argument (maintainer reason 3) in its best form.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/wlaschin-cyclic-dependencies.txt`

### 5.6 Airbnb's style guide: hoisting makes it "too easy" to call before definition. ANECDOTAL (style guide at a pinned commit)

- **Citation:** airbnb/javascript, `README.md` at `8ed19247bef145e3cc41b24a02dd1fe6d9b5e681` (21 Feb 2026), §7.1. https://github.com/airbnb/javascript#functions--declarations
- **What it says:** "Use named function expressions instead of function declarations." The reason given is that declarations are hoisted, so it's easy "to reference the function before it is defined in the file." "This harms readability and maintainability." The fix it offers for a large definition is to extract it to its own module.
- **Caveat:** an assertion with no evidence, linked to discussion issue #794. Other helpers may cover the rule's history; this entry is here only as a style guide that states the bottom-up principle for JavaScript.
- **For the post:** the clearest statement that, for JavaScript, defining before use is a readability rule. It is the direct opposite of the stepdown rule (5.1), and neither side cites a study.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/airbnb/README-8ed19247bef145e3cc41b24a02dd1fe6d9b5e681.md`

### 5.7 Kotlin and Google Java: pick an order and keep it; either direction is fine. VERIFIED (style guides at pinned commits); Vendor (JetBrains; Google)

- **Citations:** JetBrains/kotlin-web-site, `docs/topics/coding-conventions.md` at `8fedaf3d517ce063ecc677af44ca0ba7bdb2af9f`, "Class layout". google/styleguide, `javaguide.md` at `5d6fa5e1d2d33a7b080a6aef4c8e0ee5a9b358aa`, §3.4.2.
- **What they say:** Kotlin: "Do not sort the method declarations alphabetically or by visibility", but put related code together so "someone reading the class from top to bottom can follow the logic". Then: "Choose an order (either higher-level stuff first, or vice versa)" "and stick to it." Google Java: "there's no single correct recipe for how to do it"; what matters is some logical order "which its maintainer could explain if asked", and appending new methods at the end is "not a logical ordering."
- **Caveat:** guidance for languages without the hoisting question. Neither guide cites evidence.
- **For the post:** two vendor guides treat callers-first and callees-first as equally valid and ask only for consistency. That matches the research in 5.8–5.12, which finds consistent orders help a little and random order hurts, with no clear winner on direction.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/styleguides/kotlin-coding-conventions-8fedaf3d517ce063ecc677af44ca0ba7bdb2af9f.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/styleguides/google-javaguide-5d6fa5e1d2d33a7b080a6aef4c8e0ee5a9b358aa.md`

### 5.8 Geffen and Maoz, "On method ordering" (ICPC 2016): callers-first is the most common order; order affected time, not correctness. VERIFIED (author PDF, full text)

- **Citation:** Yorai Geffen, Shahar Maoz (Tel Aviv University), _On Method Ordering_, ICPC 2016, Austin, pp. 1–10. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICPC.2016.7503711 ; read: https://www.cs.tau.ac.il/~maozs/papers/methodsorder-icpc16.pdf (linked from the group page). OpenAlex lists 9 citing works.
- **Method:** four ordering strategies: StyleCop (by modifiers, then alphabetical), Calling (CL, "the caller methods appear before the callee"), Connectivity (CT, connected methods adjacent) and CL+CT. (1) A corpus study of the Qualitas Corpus (112 Java projects), 100,600 classes scored by inversion counts. (2) An online experiment: 60 engineers from mailing lists, 5 classes (142–579 LOC, 5–33 methods) each in five orders (original, SC, CL, CLCT, random), 3 multiple-choice questions per class, 328 answers.
- **What it says:**
  - The CL strategy is "inspired by the advice of the Clean Code book". The authors note "the CL strategy goes against the rules of other programming languages," "where procedures have to be defined before their use, not after," for single-pass compilation.
  - Corpus: CL scored highest, average 0.96 (median 1.0), with 79.9% of classes above 0.95. Relaxed StyleCop averaged 0.91, CT 0.95, CLCT 0.92 and full StyleCop 0.70. They conclude "a method order derived from Clean Code's ordering advice is common".
  - Experiment: "while method ordering has no clear effect on the correctness of comprehension," "it does seem to have an effect on the time engineers spent". For the first question on each class (117 answers), CLCT had over 80% correct or partly correct against about 60% for the other orders. The second and third questions (211 answers) showed no clear advantage, which the authors attribute to learning. CL and CLCT gave the lowest 25th-percentile times, and random order gave the worst 5th and 25th-percentile times.
  - Experience: "more experience implies less sensitivity to method order". Beginners (40% of the 60) were helped by SC, CL and CLCT and slowed by random order. Experts showed no such effect.
  - Power: 4.47 answers per order per class on average, "too few samples to consider statistical significance".
- **Caveat:**
  - The corpus score divides wrong pairs by all method pairs, not by the pairs a strategy constrains. With an average of 3.4 distinct local calls per class, CL constrains few pairs, so high CL scores come easily. This is my reading of their Equation 1, not their claim. The authors also say IDEs that create a new method right after its caller mean the CL result "might represent the extent use of specific tools rather than a convention".
  - The experiment used a web page with search, not an IDE, and the authors note "Real-world projects have rich IDEs to help navigation". Java only.
  - `sort-keys.md` 4.9 cites this paper from its abstract and secondhand accounts. This full text replaces that.
- **For the post:** the only experiment that varies method order with everything else fixed. Callers-first and grouped orders sped up some beginners and random order slowed them, but direction wasn't isolated: no condition put callees first with grouping held constant. It supports keeping related functions together in a consistent order more than it supports callers-first order specifically.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/geffen-maoz-icpc16.txt`, `openalex-geffen-maoz.json`, `openalex-geffen-citers.json`, `s2-geffen-citers.json`

### 5.9 Peitek, Siegmund and Apel (ICPC 2020): how far a callee sits from its call drives eye movements. VERIFIED (author PDF, full text)

- **Citation:** Norman Peitek, Janet Siegmund, Sven Apel, _What Drives the Reading Order of Programmers? An Eye Tracking Study_, ICPC 2020, pp. 342–353. https://doi.org/10.1145/3387904.3389279 ; read: https://www.se.cs.uni-saarland.de/publications/docs/PSA20.pdf
- **Method:** a non-exact replication of Busjahn et al. (5.10) and Peachock et al. (5.11). Eye tracking with "12 novice and 19 intermediate programmers". 10 Java snippets, "at most, 30 lines of code", each with a main method and up to 4 helpers, in meaningful and obfuscated versions. A new metric, source code linearity _i_, sums the line distance between each method's call and its declaration and divides by average method length. A higher _i_ means "the methods in the source code are not located in" "a position corresponding to the sequence of their calls".
- **What it says:** "the linearity of source code has an even stronger effect on reading order" "than experience, whereas the comprehension strategy has a minor effect." "Less linear source code leads to large unnecessary vertical eye movements." But "our less linear snippets tend to be more complex," so "we cannot draw robust conclusions with the conducted study". The authors call for studies of "how programmers shall organize methods in a class".
- **Caveat:** the metric uses the absolute distance between call and declaration, so it can't tell callee-above-caller from callee-below-caller. It measures distance, not direction (my reading of the formula). The snippets are tiny, and eye movement isn't comprehension.
- **For the post:** the evidence is about keeping a callee close to its call, which both orders can do. It doesn't favour hoisted declarations below their callers or `const` arrows above them.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/peitek-icpc2020.txt`, `openalex-10.1145_3387904.3389279.json`

### 5.10 Busjahn et al. (ICPC 2015): experts read code less linearly than novices, and follow execution. VERIFIED (author accepted manuscript)

- **Citation:** Teresa Busjahn, Roman Bednarik, Andrew Begel, Martha Crosby, James H. Paterson, Carsten Schulte, Bonita Sharif, Sascha Tamm, _Eye Movements in Code Reading: Relaxing the Linear Order_, ICPC 2015, pp. 255–265. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICPC.2015.36 ; read: https://researchonline.gcu.ac.uk/ws/files/24953094/ICPC2015_authors_version.pdf
- **Method:** eye tracking of 14 novices in a Java beginners' course and 6 professional developers, on short Java programs and English text. Linearity measures, plus alignment with "Story Order" (line by line) and "Execution Order".
- **What it says:** "novices read source code less linearly than natural language text." and "experts read code less linearly than novices." Novices' movements were about 80% linear on text and 70% on code, and "Expert programmers showed 60% linear eye movements,". "The Execution Order model better explains the experts'" "reading approach than Story Order does." The authors read this as experts who "tend to trace at least parts of the code."
- **Caveat:** 20 people and small programs. The sample program puts `main` at the bottom, so execution order there runs bottom-up.
- **For the post:** experienced readers don't read a file top to bottom. They follow calls. That weakens the claim that callers-first order matches how people read, since experts jump to callees wherever they are.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/busjahn-icpc2015.txt`, `openalex-10.1109_ICPC.2015.36.json`

### 5.11 Peachock et al. (HCII 2017), C++ replication of Busjahn: no novice–expert difference. UNVERIFIED (summary in Peitek et al. only)

- **Citation:** Patrick Peachock and co-authors, "Investigating Eye Movements in Natural Language and C++ Source Code – A Replication Experiment", HCII 2017, LNCS. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-319-58628-1_17 (metadata from OpenAlex; full text not opened).
- **What Peitek et al. report:** 33 students, seven short C++ snippets. Code was read less linearly than text, which replicated Busjahn, but there was "no significant difference in linearity between novice and expert participants (not replicated)". The "expert" group was advanced undergraduates.
- **For the post:** only as context for 5.10: the experience effect on linearity didn't replicate with student "experts".
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/peitek-icpc2020.txt` (lines 253–282)

### 5.12 Alharbi and Kolovos (ICPC 2024): one linear method was faster to read than the same code split into helpers. VERIFIED (arXiv full text)

- **Citation:** Seham Alharbi, Dimitris Kolovos (University of York), _Exploring the Impact of Source Code Linearity on the Programmers' Comprehension of API Code Examples_, ICPC 2024. https://doi.org/10.1145/3643916.3644395 ; arXiv:2404.02377 v1.
- **Method:** "an online controlled code comprehension experiment with 61 Java developers" from Prolific, between subjects. Linear code is "code that can be read primarily in a sequential order" "without interference from interdependent methods or classes". Non-linear versions were made by "extracting some functionalities into a set of utility methods". Linearity was graded with Peitek's _i_ (5.9).
- **What it says:** "Participants demonstrated faster reaction times when exposed to linear code examples." But "no substantial differences in correctness or subjective ratings were observed." Correctness: Mann–Whitney W = 1690, p = 0.428. The time effect was significant only when the linear example was also short. Short versus long linear examples differed significantly in comprehension (W = 125, p = 0.004) and reuse (W = 99, p = 0.000).
- **Caveat:** API usage examples (Joda-Time), not application code. Linear versus extracted mixes inlining with jump distance, so it is a study of splitting as much as of order. It cites Geffen and Maoz.
- **For the post:** jumps between helpers cost time even when they don't cost correctness. That speaks to keeping helpers few and near their use. It doesn't settle which side of the call they go on.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/arxiv-2404.02377.txt`

### 5.13 Abid et al. (ICSE 2019): in a real IDE, readers rarely follow the call graph. VERIFIED (arXiv full text)

- **Citation:** Nahla J. Abid, Bonita Sharif, Natalia Dragan, Hend Alrasheed, Jonathan I. Maletic, _Developer Reading Behavior While Summarizing Java Methods: Size and Context Matters_, ICSE 2019. https://doi.org/10.1109/ICSE.2019.00052 ; arXiv:1903.03358.
- **Method:** 18 developers (5 experts, 13 novices) summarised 63 methods from five Java systems in Eclipse with iTrace, free to scroll and switch files.
- **What it says:** "Both experts and novices read outside of the assigned" "method in 70% (213 out of 257) of the tasks." On average 94.23% of time was within the method and 5.77% outside. Most outside reading was of the lines just before or after the method in the file. The authors saw only one following the call graph: "we observed one expert and one novice switch from a statement" with a call to a location "that follows the sequence of the methods captured in" the call graph. "Searching for them by scrolling is more common than using the search option."
- **Caveat:** summarisation, not modification. The neighbours of a method are whatever the file's order puts there.
- **For the post:** what sits next to a function gets read. Callees placed far from their callers (in either direction) are rarely visited while summarising.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/arxiv-1903.03358.txt`

### 5.14 Ko, Myers, Coblenz and Aung (IEEE TSE 2006): scrolling within a file cost each developer about ten minutes. VERIFIED (author PDF); cross-reference `sort-keys.md` 4.12

- **Citation:** Amy J. Ko, Brad A. Myers, Michael J. Coblenz, Htet Htet Aung, _An Exploratory Study of How Developers Seek, Relate, and Collect Relevant Information during Software Maintenance Tasks_, IEEE TSE 32(12), pp. 971–987, 2006. https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.2006.116 ; read: https://faculty.washington.edu/ajko/papers/Ko2006SeekRelateCollect.pdf
- **Method:** the 10 most Java-experienced of 31 developers, 70-minute maintenance sessions in Eclipse, video-transcribed (see `sort-keys.md` 4.12 for the search numbers).
- **What it says:** each developer navigated 65 (±18) dependencies on average, and "few of them used Eclipse's navigation commands". 58% of navigations were of direct dependencies and 42% of indirect ones. For indirect ones, developers scrolled: "When navigating within a file using the scroll bars," "the developers had to perform visual searches for their targets," costing each developer about 10 (±4) minutes in total. All navigation overhead came to 35% of non-interrupted time.
- **Caveat:** 2006 tooling. Today's go-to-definition makes direct jumps cheap, whichever order the file uses.
- **For the post:** within-file navigation had a measured cost, but it was paid on indirect dependencies, which neither order fixes. Callers-first or callees-first only changes the direction of the scroll.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/ko-tse2006.txt`

### 5.15 Top-down and bottom-up comprehension (Brooks 1983; Pennington 1987; von Mayrhauser and Vans 1995): a different meaning of the words. VERIFIED (metadata + abstract) for von Mayrhauser and Vans; metadata only for Brooks and Pennington; definitions via Peitek et al.

- **Citations:** Ruven Brooks, "Towards a theory of the comprehension of computer programs", _IJMMS_ 18(6), pp. 543–554, 1983, https://doi.org/10.1016/S0020-7373(83)80031-5 . Nancy Pennington, "Stimulus structures and mental representations in expert comprehension of computer programs", _Cognitive Psychology_ 19(3), pp. 295–341, 1987, https://doi.org/10.1016/0010-0285(87)90007-7 . A. von Mayrhauser, A. M. Vans, "Program comprehension during software maintenance and evolution", _IEEE Computer_ 28(8), pp. 44–55, 1995, https://doi.org/10.1109/2.402076
- **What they say:** von Mayrhauser and Vans compare six models, including "Soloway, Adelson and Ehrlich's (1988) top-down model" and "Pennington's (1987) bottom-up model", and their own integrated metamodel. Peitek et al. (5.9) define the terms: "Bottom-up comprehension is used when programmers lack domain knowledge, experience, or context", while top-down is an "efficient hypothesis-driven comprehension process". In their eye tracking, the strategy had only "a minor effect" on reading order.
- **Caveat:** Brooks's and Pennington's abstracts are elided in OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar, and the full texts are Elsevier-only, so nothing of theirs is quoted.
- **For the post:** don't let "top-down" do double duty. Callers-first file order is not the same thing as top-down comprehension, and no study links them.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/openalex-10.1109_2.402076.abstract.txt`, `openalex-10.1016_S0020-7373_83_80031-5.json`, `openalex-10.1016_0010-0285_87_90007-7.json`, `s2-10.1016_S0020-7373_83_80031-5.json`, `s2-10.1016_0010-0285_87_90007-7.json`, `peitek-icpc2020.txt`

### 5.16 Uesbeck, Stefik et al. (ICSE 2016): C++ lambdas against iterators slowed students and helped no one. VERIFIED (the first author's thesis on the same experiment, full text); the ICSE paper not opened

- **Citation:** Phillip Merlin Uesbeck, Andreas Stefik, Stefan Hanenberg, Jan Pedersen, Patrick Daleiden, _An Empirical Study on the Impact of C++ Lambdas and Programmer Experience_, ICSE 2016, pp. 760–771. https://doi.org/10.1145/2884781.2884849 . Read: Uesbeck, M.S. thesis of the same title, UNLV, May 2016, https://doi.org/10.34917/9112200 (Wayback capture of the repository PDF, 6 Jan 2022). The thesis says the experiment is "described in a paper that is going to be published at ICSE" 2016 under the same title.
- **Method:** a randomized controlled trial. "Overall, 54 participants were found." That is 42 UNLV students (10 freshmen, 8 sophomores, 17 juniors, 7 seniors) and 12 professionals, after excluding 4 of 58. Participants did iteration tasks with C++ lambdas or with the iterator pattern. Measures: tasks completed, time to a correct solution, compiler errors, and time spent not compiling.
- **What it says:** "there is a slight, statistically significant, negative impact on the amount" "of time the participants took to write a program correctly" with lambdas. Time not compiling was "56.37% for the lambda group while it was 44.2% for the control group". No freshman completed the lambda tasks in time, and "only professional developers solved task at equal rates between the groups." The thesis concludes "no recommendations can be made as of yet whether to implement them."
- **Caveat:** it compares lambdas with iterators as a way to iterate, not anonymous with named functions. It is C++ syntax, and the participants were mostly students. OpenAlex attaches the thesis abstract to the ICSE DOI (see `wrong.md`).
- **For the post:** the one RCT on lambda syntax found a small cost for inexperienced programmers and none for professionals. It doesn't speak to arrows versus declarations in JavaScript.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/uesbeck-thesis-2016.txt`, `openalex-10.1145_2884781.2884849.json`, `unlv-2752.html`

### 5.17 Lucas et al. (SBES 2019; JSERD 2020): readability models say Java lambdas hurt or don't matter; developers like some conversions. VERIFIED (JSERD full text, CC BY)

- **Citation:** Walter Lucas, José Fortes, Francisco Vitor Lopes, Diego Marcílio, Rodrigo Bonifácio, Edna Dias Canedo, Fernanda Lima, João Saraiva, _Understanding the Impact of Introducing Lambda Expressions in Java Programs_, JSERD 8:7, 2020, https://doi.org/10.5753/jserd.2020.744 ; extends Lucas et al., SBES 2019, https://doi.org/10.1145/3350768.3350791 (not opened).
- **Method:** "158 pairs of code snippets" before and after introducing a lambda, from GitHub and from three refactoring tools (RJTL, NetBeans, IntelliJ). The pairs were scored with the Buse–Weimer and Posnett readability models, plus surveys: 28 practitioners in phase one, and in phase two "This phase had 182 participants located in 32 different countries".
