- **What it says:** "we could not find evidence that the introduction of lambda expressions improves" readability. For the 66 phase-one pairs, the Buse–Weimer model said lambdas "actually decreases program comprehension" (p < 0.0001), while Posnett's model found no change (p = 0.668). Developers did favour three conversions, including when "we convert anonymous inner classes to a lambda expression".
- **Caveat:** readability models are proxies, and the two disagreed. Java lambdas replace anonymous classes and loops, not named function declarations.
- **For the post:** converting to the functional syntax isn't automatically more readable, and the clear wins were where the old form was boilerplate-heavy. A JavaScript `function` declaration isn't boilerplate-heavy in that way.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/lucas-jserd2020.txt`

### 5.18 Hanenberg and Mehlhorn (EMSE 2021): untyped Java lambdas read up to 35% faster than anonymous classes, in two self-trials. VERIFIED (Wayback capture of the open-access article HTML; abstract and text)

- **Citation:** Stefan Hanenberg, Nils Mehlhorn, _Two N-of-1 self-trials on readability differences between anonymous inner classes (AICs) and lambda expressions (LEs) on Java code snippets_, EMSE 27(2), March 2022 (online 20 Dec 2021). https://doi.org/10.1007/s10664-021-10077-3 (Springer blocked direct fetches; read via the Wayback capture of 10 Sep 2025).
- **Method:** "randomized control trials executed on a single subject". Two experiments asked the subject to count parameters, or used parameters, in listener code written as an AIC or an LE. "The results are based on 9,600 observations".
- **What it says:** "LEs without type annotations require less reading time", a "reduction of reaction time of at most 35%". But "the effect seems to be so small," with no expectation of "a larger impact on daily programming".
- **Caveat:** one subject per trial (the authors), a counting task, and Java AICs, which are far more verbose than any JavaScript function form.
- **For the post:** the one timed result in favour of terse function syntax compares it with a much heavier form, and the authors themselves call the effect small.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/hanenberg-mehlhorn-emse2021.txt`

### 5.19 Mazinanian et al. (OOPSLA 2017): developers adopt lambdas for terseness; lambdas also drop signature information. VERIFIED (author PDF)

- **Citation:** Davood Mazinanian, Ameya Ketkar, Nikolaos Tsantalis, Danny Dig, _Understanding the Use of Lambda Expressions in Java_, PACMPL 1(OOPSLA), article 85, 2017. https://doi.org/10.1145/3133909 ; read: https://users.encs.concordia.ca/~nikolaos/publications/OOPSLA_2017.pdf
- **Method:** 100,540 lambdas in 241 projects (19,770 contributors), plus "firehouse" interviews with 97 developers who had just added lambdas.
- **What it says:** "Most of the developers believe that lambda expressions are more readable" than the equivalent code, mainly anonymous inner classes. Terseness was the most frequent theme (57 mentions). But "two developers mentioned that this terseness actually hampers readability." The authors warn that "the use of lambdas eliminates important information from the source code," such as a `@NotNull` annotation on the interface method's signature. 89.94% of lambdas are passed as arguments, and 60% are in test code.
- **Caveat:** usage and opinion, not comprehension. Java.
- **For the post:** developers choose terse function forms because they like them, which matches the maintainer's stated preference. The annotation point is the Java version of typing a function through its variable (maintainer reason 2): the types move to the variable's type and out of the function's own signature.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/mazinanian-oopsla2017.txt`, `openalex-10.1145_3133909.json`

### 5.20 Torres et al. (JSS 2023): a concise arrow was not a JavaScript atom of confusion. VERIFIED (publisher version from the Utrecht University repository)

- **Citation:** Adriano Torres, Caio Oliveira, Márcio Okimoto, Diego Marcílio, Pedro Queiroga, Fernando Castor, Rodrigo Bonifácio, Edna Dias Canedo, Márcio Ribeiro, Eduardo Monteiro, _An Investigation of confusing code patterns in JavaScript_, JSS 203, article 111731, Sept 2023. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.jss.2023.111731 ; read: https://dspace.library.uu.nl/handle/1874/431585
- **Method:** a mining study of 72 projects, two experiments and interviews. In experiment 1, "This study had 70 participants." (median four years' programming, and "the median experience with JavaScript programming is one year"). Each predicted the output of a clean and an obfuscated version of 24 candidate atoms. The arrow candidate is "The usage of arrow function notation, instead of the more" "traditional JavaScript notations for anonymous functions." In the materials, the obfuscated version is `x => x * x * x` and the clean one is `(x) => { return x * x * x; }`.
- **What it says:** arrow function: 54 of 70 correct on the concise form and 58 of 70 on the block form (+7%). McNemar p = 0.2891 against a Bonferroni threshold of 0.0021, so not significant. "eight of the 24 analyzed atom candidates can be considered atoms of confusion", and arrow functions weren't among them. Experiment 2 didn't test arrows. In the interviews, arrow functions came up among "other idioms that are JavaScript atom candidates," illustrated with `(x) => x + 1` against `function (x) { return x + 1 }`.
- **Caveat:** one trivial snippet per form, output prediction, mostly people with little JavaScript experience. Both versions in the experiment are arrows, so it tests concise against block bodies and parentheses, not arrow against `function`.
- **For the post:** the only JavaScript-specific measurement found that touches function syntax. A concise body cost 4 correct answers in 70, which wasn't significant. That is weak evidence either way for `arrow-body-style` and nothing on `func-style`.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/torres-jss2023.layout.txt`, `torres-jss2023.txt`

### 5.21 Gallaba, Mesbah and Beschastnikh (ESEM 2015): 43% of JavaScript callback call sites are anonymous. VERIFIED (author copy on Zenodo)

- **Citation:** Keheliya Gallaba, Ali Mesbah, Ivan Beschastnikh, _Don't Call Us, We'll Call You: Characterizing Callbacks in JavaScript_, ESEM 2015. https://doi.org/10.1109/ESEM.2015.7321196 ; read: https://zenodo.org/record/3586025
- **What it says:** across "138 JavaScript programs, with over 5 million lines of JavaScript code.", every 10th function definition takes a callback and "over 43% of all callback-accepting function callsites are anonymous." The authors argue "callbacks induce a non-linear control flow" and that anonymous, nested callbacks are hard to understand, which is an assertion, not a measurement.
- **Caveat:** 2015, before arrow functions were common, and usage rather than comprehension. Other helpers may cover adoption of arrows.
- **For the post:** context that inline anonymous functions were already the majority idiom for callbacks before arrows. `prefer-arrow-callback` changes their syntax, not their count.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/gallaba-esem2015.txt`

### 5.22 Negative findings: what no study measures. Searched; nothing found

- **No study compares `function` declarations with `const` arrow or function expressions** in JavaScript or TypeScript for comprehension, errors, bug-proneness or review time. The nearest are 5.16–5.19 (C++ and Java lambdas against iterators or anonymous classes) and 5.20 (concise against block arrows).
- **No study compares callers-first with callees-first order with grouping held constant.** Geffen and Maoz (5.8) had no callees-first condition. Peitek (5.9) and Alharbi and Kolovos (5.12) vary distance and splitting, not direction.
- **No study of concise against block bodies** beyond Torres et al.'s single pair (5.20).
- **No eye-tracking or navigation study of JavaScript files ordered around hoisting** (declarations used before definition, against `const` functions defined first).
- **Searches:** OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar for papers citing Geffen and Maoz (13 found; the relevant ones are 5.9, 5.12 and `sort-keys.md` 4.10–4.11). OpenAlex keyword searches on arrow functions, lambda readability, anonymous function readability and JavaScript atoms of confusion. Web searches on arrow-function comprehension and readability.

## 6. Bugs and costs

Items 6.1–6.5 are runtime errors that are specific to function style: a cycle that only breaks with `const` exports, compilers and bundlers that turn a hoisted declaration into a `const`/`let` (or a `const` into a `var`), and a tool that didn't model hoisting. All are single reports (ANECDOTAL). I found no large project's style-migration PR that recorded TDZ breakage (see `dropped.md`). 6.6–6.12 are bug studies: none has a hoisting or TDZ category, and the two that look for `this` binding find little. 6.13–6.16 are engine-team sources on cost: the only measured difference between the styles is TDZ checks on `const`/`let` bindings. 6.17 is stack traces. GitHub JSON is in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/`, papers in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/`, V8 material in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/`, esbuild changelogs in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/esbuild/` at `v0.28.2`.

### 6.1 Next.js #58778: exported arrow components throw a TDZ error through a barrel cycle; declarations work. ANECDOTAL (GitHub issue)

- **Citation:** vercel/next.js #58778, "Regular exported arrow function client components throw 'cannot access before initialization' error message," opened 22 Nov 2023 (Next.js 14.0.3), closed as stale 20 Aug 2025. https://github.com/vercel/next.js/issues/58778
- **What it says:** two client components import each other through an `index` file. With `export const ClientComponent1 = () => ...` the page throws "ReferenceError: Cannot access 'x' before initialization". The reporter: it "worked using either regular function syntax or" a `var` arrow. A second user: "after converting to a function declaration the error goes away."
- **Caveat:** one report with no maintainer diagnosis. The cause is very likely an import cycle through the barrel (`import { ClientComponent2 } from "."`), where function declarations are initialised at link time and `const` exports are not (1.15; `import-cycles.md` 2.3, 2.8).
- **For the post:** the clearest real case where switching declarations to `const` arrows turns a latent cycle into a crash. Pairs with Ultracite's barrel and cycle rules: the arrow style makes those rules matter more.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/vercel_next.js-58778.json`

### 6.2 Next.js #98820: React Compiler lowers a nested function declaration to a `const` and creates a TDZ error. ANECDOTAL (GitHub issue, open)

- **Citation:** vercel/next.js #98820, "React Compiler + Turbopack: lazy useState initializer throws TDZ when helper function declarations appear later in the same hook," opened 17 Sep 2026 (Next.js 16.3.5, React 19.2.8), open, labels SWC and Turbopack. https://github.com/vercel/next.js/issues/98820
- **What it says:** a hook calls `useState(getItem)` above `function parseItem` and `function getItem`. That "is valid JavaScript (nested `function` declarations hoist)," but React Compiler "lowers `parseItem` to a `const` while leaving `useState(getItem)` above it." Result: "Cannot access 'parseItem' before initialization". It doesn't reproduce with the compiler off. A commenter reports the TypeScript-based compiler in the React playground produces the same output, so `babel-plugin-react-compiler` users would be affected too.
- **Caveat:** single report; the commenter's root-cause claim is not confirmed by a maintainer.
- **For the post:** a compiler that treats a function declaration as if it were a `const` reintroduces exactly the ordering problem the source avoided. Code already written in `const` style, top to bottom, is immune to this class of transform bug.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/vercel_next.js-98820.json`

### 6.3 esbuild #4533: a function declaration in a `switch` case is rewritten to a `let` and throws. ANECDOTAL (GitHub issue, open)

- **Citation:** evanw/esbuild #4533, "Function declaration in a switch case is lowered to a TDZ-bound let initializer," opened 1 Sep 2026 against 0.28.2, no replies. https://github.com/evanw/esbuild/issues/4533
- **What it says:** `case 0: console.log(g()); case 1: function g() {...}` becomes `let g2 = function() {...}` in `case 1`, so `case 0` throws `Cannot access 'g2' before initialization`, with and without minify. The reporter explains that the original binding is set "by `BlockDeclarationInstantiation` when entering the entire `CaseBlock`," and that "A function declaration and a `let` declaration" "with a function-expression initializer do not have the same initialization timing."
- **Caveat:** an edge case (sloppy-mode block function in a `switch`), unconfirmed by the maintainer.
- **For the post:** a compact demonstration of 1.11: a declaration and a `let` holding a function expression are not interchangeable, even for a bundler.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/evanw_esbuild-4533.json`

### 6.4 Next.js #57392: server actions didn't respect hoisted function declarations. ANECDOTAL (GitHub issue)

- **Citation:** vercel/next.js #57392, "Server actions doesn't work with hoisted function declarations," opened 25 Oct 2023 (Next.js 13.5.6). https://github.com/vercel/next.js/issues/57392
- **What it says:** a `'use server'` function declared after the `return` of a component is used above it. "This should work because it would work in normal JS." The transform inserted its `createActionProxy` call "after the use and after the `return` statement." A Next.js team member later noted a fix PR left variables that "can still be 'captured' before they're actually initialized."
- **Caveat:** single report; framework-specific transform.
- **For the post:** tools that rewrite functions have to model hoisting, and sometimes don't. Writing functions above their use (what `no-use-before-define` enforces) avoids depending on that.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/vercel_next.js-57392.json`

### 6.5 esbuild turns top-level `let`/`const`/`class` into `var` when bundling, for speed. VERIFIED (esbuild changelogs at v0.28.2)

- **Citation:** esbuild CHANGELOG-2020.md (0.7.19, 0.8.13) and CHANGELOG-2023.md (0.18.4), at tag `v0.28.2`. https://github.com/evanw/esbuild/blob/v0.28.2/CHANGELOG-2020.md
- **What it says:**
  - 0.7.19 added `--avoid-tdz` after WebKit bug 199866 (6.15): "the initialization phase of the bundle produced by esbuild runs 10x faster in Safari" if top-level `const`, `let` and `class` become `var`. "It's a difference between a loading time of about 2sec vs. about 200ms." It was opt-in because "it changes the semantics of the code".
  - 0.8.13: the option "is now always applied during bundling", turning top-level `let`, `const`, and `class` into `var`; "Now this behavior will always happen," and "there is no way to turn it off."
  - 0.18.4 (2023) gives a second reason, lazily evaluated modules, and repeats that "multiple JavaScript VMs have had and continue to have performance issues with TDZ", citing V8 issue 13723 (6.13).
- **Caveat:** I did not run esbuild (not installed in the worktree) to confirm current output. The first measurement is on "a large private code base".
- **For the post:** in an esbuild bundle, a top-level `const` arrow called too early becomes `undefined`, so the error you see in production may be `TypeError: x is not a function`, not the `ReferenceError` you see in tests. The TDZ's safety net is partly removed by the most common bundler.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/esbuild/CHANGELOG-2020@v0.28.2.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/esbuild/CHANGELOG-2023@v0.28.2.md`

### 6.6 Ocariza, Pattabiraman and Zorn, "JavaScript errors in the wild: an empirical study" (ISSRE 2011): no hoisting category. VERIFIED (author copy)

- **Citation:** Frolin S. Ocariza Jr., Karthik Pattabiraman and Benjamin Zorn, "JavaScript Errors in the Wild: An Empirical Study," ISSRE 2011, pp. 100–109, doi:10.1109/ISSRE.2011.28. Author copy: https://people.ece.ubc.ca/~frolino/projects/jser/web2_0_paper.pdf
- **Method:** console error messages from 50 of the Alexa top 100 sites, collected while interacting with them; errors categorised by message.
- **What it says:** "About 93%" "of the errors fall into one of four categories:" "Permission Denied (52%), Undefined Symbol (28%), Null Exception" "(9%), and Syntax Errors (4%)." Undefined Symbol covers code that "calls a function that has not been defined," among other things.
- **Caveat:** pre-ES2015, so no TDZ was possible; categories come from error messages, not root causes, so a hoisting mistake would land in Undefined Symbol or Null Exception without being identified. The talk slides (secondary) say 94%; the paper says about 93%.
- **For the post:** there's no evidence here either way on hoisting. Don't cite it as showing use-before-define is common.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/ocariza-issre11.txt`

### 6.7 Ocariza, Bajaj, Pattabiraman and Mesbah, "An empirical study of client-side JavaScript bugs" (ESEM 2013): 18 and 15 of 317. VERIFIED (author copy); TSE 2016 extension VERIFIED (author copy)

- **Citation:** F. S. Ocariza Jr., K. Bajaj, K. Pattabiraman, A. Mesbah, "An Empirical Study of Client-Side JavaScript Bugs," ESEM 2013, doi:10.1109/ESEM.2013.18. Author copy: https://people.ece.ubc.ca/~frolino/docs/js_bugs_study_paper.pdf . Extension: "A Study of Causes and Consequences of Client-Side JavaScript Bugs," IEEE TSE 2016, https://people.ece.ubc.ca/frolino/docs/jsbugsstudy_journal.pdf
- **Method:** "We perform an empirical study of 317 bug reports from" "12 bug repositories." (8 web apps, 4 libraries), classified by error and failure.
- **What it says:** Table III: Undefined/Null Variable Usage 18/317, Undefined Method 15/317, Incorrect Return Value 10, Syntax-Based Fault 34, Other 5, Incorrect Method Parameter 235 (207 DOM-related). "The majority (65%) of JavaScript faults are DOM-related,". The TSE extension (502 reports, 19 repositories) gives 27 and 19 for the first two categories out of 502.
- **Caveat:** no category for hoisting, scope, TDZ or `this`. "Undefined Method" (a call to a function that has not been defined) is the closest and is about 5% (15/317), but it is not broken down by cause.
- **For the post:** in the best-known JavaScript bug taxonomy, mistakes the function-style rules target are too rare or too mixed to get their own category.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/ocariza-esem13-layout.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/ocariza-tse16-layout.txt`

### 6.8 Hanam, Brito and Mesbah, "Discovering bug patterns in JavaScript" (FSE 2016): `this` binding is one of 13 patterns. VERIFIED (author copy)

- **Citation:** Quinn Hanam, Fernando S. de M. Brito and Ali Mesbah, "Discovering Bug Patterns in JavaScript," FSE 2016, doi:10.1145/2950290.2950308. Author copy: https://people.ece.ubc.ca/amesbah/resources/papers/fse16.pdf
- **Method:** "a large-scale study of" "common bug patterns by mining 105K commits from 134" "server-side JavaScript projects." Clustering of AST-level bug-fix change types, 219 change types, 13 patterns.
- **What it says:** pattern §5.4.5, "this not correctly bound": "developers may incorrectly assume that this is bound" "to the object in which the function is defined." Two fixes: "Access this through closure (2 CTs)", i.e. `var self = this`, and "Bind this (1 CT)" with `call`, `bind` or `apply`. No pattern for hoisting or use before definition; the largest group is dereferenced non-values.
- **Caveat:** per-pattern commit counts are in the authors' online dataset, which I didn't open; the paper text I have doesn't give them. Node.js code from about 2015, before arrows were common.
- **For the post:** the one study that names a `this`-binding pattern found it among the smaller ones. Arrows fix exactly this pattern, which supports `prefer-arrow-callback` far more than it supports arrows for top-level functions.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/hanam-fse16.txt`

### 6.9 Gyimesi et al., BugsJS (ICST 2019): 0 of 453 bugs were wrong-`this` bugs. VERIFIED (ICST paper); STVR taxonomy not opened

- **Citation:** Péter Gyimesi, Béla Vancsics, Andrea Stocco, Davood Mazinanian, Árpád Beszédes, Rudolf Ferenc, Ali Mesbah, "BugsJS: a Benchmark of JavaScript Bugs," ICST 2019, doi:10.1109/ICST.2019.00019. https://bugsjs.github.io/paper/ICST19.pdf
- **Method:** 453 manually validated bugs from 10 Node.js projects; four authors classified each fix into Hanam et al.'s categories (6.8) and Pan et al.'s fix types.
- **What it says:** Table V: "Dereferenced non-values" 35, "Incorrect API config" 3, "Incorrect comparison" 2, "Unhanded exceptions" 2, "Missing arguments" 0, "Incorrect this bounding" ("Accessing a wrong this reference") 0. Across all 453 bugs only 42 fit Hanam's categories. New fix pattern "Variable declaration" (2): a variable used without declaring it, which the paper says is "'hoisted' to the top of the global scope."
- **Caveat:** that "hoisted" is a misnomer (it describes an implicit global, not hoisting; see `wrong.md`). The 2021 STVR extension with the full taxonomy is 10.4 MB, over the limit, and the Wiley page returned 403, so I couldn't check it.
- **For the post:** in a benchmark of real Node.js bugs, `this` binding mistakes didn't appear at all, and neither did use-before-definition.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/gyimesi-icst19-layout.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/gyimesi-icst19.txt`

### 6.10 Gao, Bird and Barr, "To type or not to type" (ICSE 2017): 60 of 400; no scope or hoisting category. VERIFIED (author copy); Vendor (Microsoft Research co-author)

- **Citation:** Zheng Gao, Christian Bird, Earl T. Barr, "To Type or Not to Type: Quantifying Detectable Bugs in JavaScript," ICSE 2017, doi:10.1109/ICSE.2017.75. https://earlbarr.com/publications/typestudy.pdf
- **What it says:** "both Flow 0.30 and TypeScript 2.0 successfully detect 15%!" Of 400 public bugs, "Flow successfully detects 59 of them and TypeScript 58", 60 each after the unknowns were resolved (95% interval 11.5%–18.5%). The taxonomy of undetected bugs is StringError, BranchError, PredError, UIError, SpecError, RegexError, ResError, APIError and JavaScript's built-in error types. There is no scope, hoisting or `this` category.
- **For the post:** relevant to the maintainer's typing reason (2) only indirectly: the study measures types, not how functions are declared, and says nothing about function style.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/gao-typestudy-icse17.txt`

### 6.11 Pradel and Sen, DeepBugs (OOPSLA 2018): name-based bugs only. VERIFIED (author copy)

- **Citation:** Michael Pradel and Koushik Sen, "DeepBugs: A Learning Approach to Name-Based Bug Detection," OOPSLA 2018, doi:10.1145/3276517. https://software-lab.org/publications/oopsla2018_DeepBugs.pdf
- **What it says:** trained on "a corpus of 150,000 JavaScript files", detectors for swapped arguments, wrong binary operator and wrong operand; inspection found 102 real bugs and code quality problems. No detector or category for hoisting, scope or `this`.
- **For the post:** not evidence for or against either style; list it only to show the gap.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/pradel-deepbugs-oopsla18.txt`

### 6.12 Gallaba, Mesbah and Beschastnikh, callbacks study (ESEM 2015): 43% of callback call sites pass an anonymous function. VERIFIED (author copy)

- **Citation:** Keheliya Gallaba, Ali Mesbah, Ivan Beschastnikh, "Don't Call Us, We'll Call You: Characterizing Callbacks in JavaScript," ESEM 2015. https://www.cs.ubc.ca/~bestchai/papers/esem15-js-callbacks-study.pdf
- **Method:** static analysis of 138 JavaScript programs, over 5 million lines.
- **What it says:** "Over 43% of all callback-accepting function" "callsites are invoked with at least one anonymous callback." Named callbacks "can be reused and are easily identified in stack" "traces or breakpoints during debugging activities." The paper also claims naming "causes the callback function to persist in memory and" "prevents it from being garbage collected."
- **Caveat:** the memory claim has no citation and no engine source supports it (see `wrong.md`). Pre-arrow code mostly.
- **For the post:** anonymous callbacks are common, and that is where unnamed frames come from (6.17), not from top-level `const` functions.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/gallaba-esem15-layout.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/papers/gallaba-esem15.txt`

### 6.13 V8: `let`/`const` slower than `var` because of TDZ checks that function hoisting makes hard to remove. VERIFIED (issue thread mirror, V8 commits, TC39 notes) plus my bytecode check; Vendor (Google)

- **Citation:** V8 issue 13723, "let/const are 10% slower than var," opened 8 Feb 2023 by a Bloomberg engineer, mirrored on the v8-reviews Google Group, https://groups.google.com/g/v8-reviews/c/drZ8jJcE5Xo (original https://bugs.chromium.org/p/v8/issues/detail?id=13723); V8 commits `da0bcffa` (12 Apr 2023, "[interpreter] Elide more TDZ hole checks"), `5593d76d` (19 May 2023, "Enable TDZ elision by default"), revert and reland `c17ee553` (26 Jun 2025); TC39 notes 8 and 10 Oct 2024.
- **What it says:**
  - The report: compiling TypeScript's compiler with `let`/`const` turned into `var` gives "a significant reduction of ~10% (between 8% and 12%)" in parse time on Node 18, while Bun/JSC was unaffected.
  - A V8 engineer (comment 8; Shu-yu Guo later wrote the fix): uses of closed-over variables "cannot have their hole checks elided, because of function declaration hoisting." So "the current state is that _each_ use a context let/const has a hole check". Another (comment 14): "the hole check is not statically possible to eliminate without some serious static analysis," "due to function declaration hoisting."
  - The fix keeps only the first check per basic block. Comment 16 measured "a 3-4% improvement in tsc's 'Parsing time' metric", against 7% from TypeScript's manual change to `var`.
  - TC39, 10 Oct 2024: Shu-yu Guo said Google teams that "try to stop transpiling to ES5 and got performance regressions" were one motivation for the JS0 proposal, and "this is where the TDZ stuff came from." 8 Oct 2024, Waldemar Horwat: "There is nothing really inherently expensive about the TDZ." "It's a problem for implementers due to excessive forward hoisting within scopes,".
  - My check (Node 24.14.1, V8 13.6, 29 Oct 2026, `--print-bytecode`): a function that calls a module-level `const` arrow twice emits one `ThrowReferenceErrorIfHole` (the second call's check is elided); the same calls to a function declaration emit none. In a `for...of` loop the check sits inside the loop body; in an `if`/`else`, one per branch.
- **Caveat:** my check shows interpreter bytecode only; I didn't measure whether Maglev or TurboFan remove the checks in optimised code, or the time they cost. The 10% figure is for `var` vs `let`/`const` on all variables in a large compiler, not for function bindings alone. TC39 notes are transcriptions.
- **For the post:** the one measured runtime cost of `func-style: expression`: a top-level `const` function referenced from other functions needs a TDZ check per call site in V8's interpreter, and a declaration doesn't. It's small per call and partly elided since 2023, but it's real, and it's caused by exactly the hoisting the rule avoids.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/v8-reviews-13723.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/commit-da0bcffa3d.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/commit-5593d76d69.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/commit-c17ee553a8.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/tc39-notes/meetings_2024-10_october-10.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/src/tc39-notes/meetings_2024-10_october-08.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/tdz-bytecode-node24.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/tdz-bytecode-node24-loop.txt`

### 6.14 TypeScript switched its own closure state from `let`/`const` to `var` to avoid TDZ checks (2023). VERIFIED (PRs); Vendor (Microsoft)

- **Citation:** microsoft/TypeScript #52656 (Jake Bailey, "[experiment] Run babel over output for let/const to var," opened 7 Feb 2023, closed unmerged); #52832 (Daniel Rosenwasser, "Switch let/const to var in the scanner & parser for top-levelish variables," merged 18 Feb 2023); #52835 ("Swap closure state in the type-checker to `var` to avoid TDZ checks," merged 22 Feb 2023).
- **What it says:** #52832: moving from ES5 to ES2018 output lost performance, "a significant part due to TDZ checks that engines need to perform." Switching "top-level shared closure variables" to `var` removes them, and is "fairly safe, since our functions follow the same initialization mechanism". In #52656 Bailey: "Yep, it's TDZ." and the gain "was 5-9%, and now it's more like 7-15%"; Bun showed no such effect.
- **Caveat:** these are mutable state variables (`let`), not function bindings. TypeScript's own functions are declarations.
- **For the post:** a large, performance-sensitive codebase chose `var` over `let` for hot closure state on measured TDZ cost. It shows the mechanism is real at scale; it does not measure `const` functions specifically.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/ts-pr-52656.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/ts-pr-52656-comments.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/ts-pr-52832.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/gh/ts-pr-52835.json`

### 6.15 JavaScriptCore had a TDZ-tracking bug that made large bundles load 10x slower; fixed in October 2020. VERIFIED (WebKit Bugzilla); Vendor (Apple)

- **Citation:** WebKit bug 199866, "Better cache our serialization of the outer TDZ environment when creating FunctionExecutables during bytecode generation," reported 17 Jul 2019, fixed in r269115 (28 Oct 2020). https://bugs.webkit.org/show_bug.cgi?id=199866
- **What it says:** a 5 MB Dart-compiled bundle froze Safari for about 10 seconds when loaded as a module or inside a wrapper function. A JSC engineer (Keith Miller, 22 Jan 2020): "the problem is in how we track if we need a TDZ check." Switching to `var` "ran instantly in about 300ms in the JSC CLI." A second repro, titled "Repro for 10x slower initialization time in Figma", was attached on 20 Oct 2020; the fix landed as "Committed r269115".
- **Caveat:** fixed six years ago; relevant as history and as the origin of esbuild's `var` conversion (6.5).
- **For the post:** TDZ cost has been an engine-specific bug, not a constant; avoid claims that `const` is slow in general.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/webkit-bug-199866.txt`

### 6.16 Lazy parsing and ES2015 performance: no engine source shows a speed or memory difference between declarations, expressions and arrows. VERIFIED (v8.dev); Vendor (Google, Mozilla, Apple)

- **Citation:** Toon Verwaest and Marja Hölttä, "Blazingly fast parsing, part 2: lazy parsing," v8.dev, 15 Apr 2019, https://v8.dev/blog/preparser ; "High-performance ES2015 and beyond," v8.dev, 17 Feb 2017, https://v8.dev/blog/high-performance-es2015 ; Jason Orendorff, "ES6 In Depth: Arrow functions," Mozilla Hacks, 4 Jun 2015, https://hacks.mozilla.org/2015/06/es6-in-depth-arrow-functions/ ; "JSC 💕 ES6," WebKit blog, https://webkit.org/blog/7536/jsc-loves-es6/
- **What it says:**
  - V8 preparses every function form lazily. The one form-specific heuristic is for parenthesised function expressions: "If a function is a parenthesized function expression," V8 assumes "it will be called" and "V8 eagerly compiles PIFEs".
  - The 2017 ES2015 post reports "a clear trend towards parity of ES2015+ and ES5." It doesn't mention arrow functions.
  - Orendorff (Mozilla) gives the style rule from the engine side: "Use non-arrow functions for methods that will be called using the object.method() syntax." "Use arrow functions for everything else." No performance claim.
  - The WebKit ES6 post discusses generators, classes, destructuring and others; it gives no arrow-specific result.
- **Caveat:** absence of evidence: I found no engine-team post or doc measuring call speed or memory for declarations vs expressions vs arrows. Arrows skip creating a `prototype` object (1.13), but I found no engine source quantifying that.
- **For the post:** say plainly that, apart from TDZ checks on `const` bindings (6.13), there's no measured performance difference on record between the styles.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/v8dev-blog_preparser.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/v8dev-blog_high-performance-es2015.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/mozhacks-es6-in-depth-arrow-functions.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/webkit-7536-jsc-loves-es6.txt`

### 6.17 Stack traces: V8 names frames from `name` or an inferred name; JSC shows `<anonymous>` more often. VERIFIED (V8 docs) plus my check; Vendor (Google)

- **Citation:** V8, "Stack trace API," https://v8.dev/docs/stack-trace-api (fetched 29 Oct 2026); ECMA-262 §20.2.4.2 (1.12).
- **What it says:** `getFunctionName` "returns the name of the current function, typically its name property." "If a name property is not available" "an attempt is made to infer a name" from the function's context. "If neither is available <anonymous> is used as the name." The API "is specific to V8 and is not supported by any other JavaScript implementations."
  - My check (Node 24.14.1 and Bun 1.4.4, 29 Oct 2026): `function declared`, `const constArrow = () =>`, `const constFnExpr = function`, an object-literal arrow `prop`, and a destructuring default all show their names in both engines. `holder.assigned = function () {}` has `name === ""`; V8 still prints `at holder.assigned` (inferred), JSC prints `at <anonymous>`. An arrow passed through a call has no name in either engine and appears as a bare location.
- **Caveat:** two engines, one small file; Firefox not checked. Minifiers that rename or drop names change all of this, whatever the source style.
- **For the post:** `const` arrows don't hurt stack traces; the frames that lose names are inline callbacks and member assignments, which `func-style` doesn't govern (`func-names` and `prefer-arrow-callback` do).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/v8/v8dev-docs_stack-trace-api.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/names-and-traces-node24.txt`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/A/text/names-and-traces-bun.txt`

## 7. What developers use and prefer

What measured data exists on which function style developers use or prefer, and what maintainers of large projects said when they chose or changed a style. 7.1–7.3 are measurements (State of JS, Lucas et al. 2025, Tómasdóttir et al. 2020); 7.4–7.9 are maintainer decisions and discussions (Node.js, Deno, Vue, and the lint settings of other large projects), all ANECDOTAL; 7.10 is a qualitative study of style-guide debates. No source measured preference between declarations and `const` functions directly. Survey data is in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/stateofjs/` and `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/stateofjs/`, papers in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/lucas2025/`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/tomasdottir/`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/depaz2022/`, issue and PR JSON in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/`, project configs in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/`.

### 7.1 State of JS asked about arrow functions only in 2019 and 2020, as used, heard or never heard: 97.0% and 97.9% had used them. VERIFIED (question files, results JSON)

- **Citation:** Devographics/surveys `state_of_js/js2016`–`js2026/questions.yml` at `9e74982b`; results from https://2020.stateofjs.com/page-data/en-US/features/syntax/page-data.json (fetched 29 Oct 2026).
- **Method:** the "syntax" feature block in 2019 and 2020 had `arrow_functions` with buckets `used`, `heard`, `never_heard`. From 2021 the item is commented out under `# removed in 2021`.
- **What it says:** 2019: 20,359 of 20,986 respondents to the item had used arrow functions (97.0%), 455 had heard of them, 172 hadn't. 2020: 22,226 of 22,695 (97.9%), 321, 148. No edition from 2016 to 2026 asks about function declarations versus expressions, hoisting, or `func-style`.
- **Caveat:** "used" means ever used, by self-selected survey takers. It says nothing about whether they prefer arrows for named functions.
- **For the post:** say plainly that the largest JavaScript survey never asked about function style; the only datum is that almost everyone has used arrows.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/stateofjs/js2019-questions.yml`, `js2020-questions.yml`, `js2021-questions.yml`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/stateofjs/2020-page-data_en-US_features_syntax_page-data.json`

### 7.2 Lucas et al., "Understanding the adoption of modern Javascript features" (EMSE 2025): arrows are common, but declarations weren't measured. VERIFIED (full text, repository copy)

- **Citation:** Walter Lucas, Rafael Nunes, Rodrigo Bonifácio, Fausto Carvalho, Ricardo Lima, Michael Silva, Adriano Torres, Paola Accioly, Eduardo Monteiro, João Saraiva, _Empirical Software Engineering_ 30(4), article 107, 5 May 2025, https://doi.org/10.1007/s10664-025-10663-9 ; read: https://hdl.handle.net/1822/95662 (Universidade do Minho repository PDF; Springer's PDF link returned an HTML page).
- **Method:** "we mined the source code history of 158 JavaScript" open-source projects created at least three years before ES2015 ("158 out of 270849 JavaScript projects (0.05%)"), counted modern features over time, and read code review comments.
- **What it says:** arrow functions ("Arrow Function Declarations" in the paper, meaning any arrow) are among features used by more than 75% of the 158 projects; the median project has 121 occurrences, and the median share of files using them is 17.39%. One project (Handsontable) has 18,569 arrows, 16.9% of all found. Review comments cite conciseness and lexical `this` for adopting them, and one reviewer worried arrows "could potentially raise the learning curve,".
- **Caveat:** the study doesn't count function declarations or `const`-assigned functions, so it can't say which style projects use for named functions; callbacks dominate any arrow count.
- **For the post:** the one recent adoption study confirms arrows are everywhere and is silent on the question the post asks. Don't cite it as evidence for either style.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/lucas2025/lucas-uminho.txt`, `lucas-uminho-layout.txt`, `lucas-uminho.pdf`

### 7.3 Tómasdóttir, Aniche and van Deursen (IEEE TSE 2020): `func-style`, `func-names` and `no-use-before-define` are among the most-disabled rules. VERIFIED (accepted manuscript via Wayback); see also `length-limits.md` 4.69

- **Citation:** Kristín Fjóla Tómasdóttir, Maurício Aniche, Arie van Deursen, _The Adoption of JavaScript Linters in Practice: A Case Study on ESLint_, IEEE TSE 46(8), 863–891, 2020, https://doi.org/10.1109/TSE.2018.2871058 ; read: http://web.archive.org/web/20240416135835/https://pure.tudelft.nl/ws/files/46810031/tse_js.pdf
- **Method:** "we analyzed 9,548 ESLint configuration files" "extracted from 86,366 JavaScript projects on GitHub." ("As of February 2017."); 6,413 used a preset, 3,135 didn't (Table 8). Tables 11–12 list the top 20 enabled and disabled rules per group.
- **What it says:** disabled, no preset (n=3,135): `func-names` 681 (21.8%, rank 4), `no-use-before-define` 581 (18.6%, rank 9), `func-style` 549 (17.5%, rank 14). Disabled, with a preset (n=6,413): `func-names` 430 (6.7%, rank 4), `no-use-before-define` 358 (5.6%, rank 7), `arrow-body-style` 224 (3.5%, rank 16). Enabled, with a preset: `no-use-before-define` 419 (6.5%, rank 15). `func-style` and `prefer-arrow-callback` aren't in any enabled list. The paper's only comment: "Other rules include func-names to" "require or disallow named function expressions for debugging purposes,".
- **Caveat:** 2017 data. The most used presets were `eslint:recommended` (2,226) and Airbnb's two presets (1,723 together), 56.7% of all preset uses (Table 7); Airbnb enables `func-names` and `no-use-before-define`, so for preset users _disabled_ mostly means overriding Airbnb. Without one, a rule set to `off` is often from a generated config that lists every rule, so it's weak evidence of active rejection. The printed percentages for the no-preset group fit a denominator of about 3,130, not the stated 3,135; the difference is small.
- **For the post:** the one large study of real ESLint configs shows people switching these rules off more than on, with `no-use-before-define` the order rule preset users override most. It doesn't say which style they then wrote.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/tomasdottir/tse_js.txt`, `tse_js-layout.txt`

### 7.4 Node.js core counted its own code in 2017: 2,344 declarations, 77 expressions, and required declarations "to avoid potential confusion over the Temporal Dead Zone". ANECDOTAL (maintainer's count and PR)

- **Citation:** nodejs/node PR #12711 "tools: require function declarations" (Rich Trott, opened 28 Apr 2017; landed as `a180259e` and `aea7269c`, 1 May 2017); commit `aea7269c`.
- **What it says:** "Welp, not sure how this will be received," the PR opens; reviewers were nitting new contributors "to avoid potential confusion over the Temporal Dead Zone". The commit: "This is the predominant style in our" "code base (77 instances of expressions to 2344 instances of" "declarations)." The PR first rewrote the expressions, mostly in tests, then added `func-style: declaration` with arrows allowed. The PR's comments contain no objections (I didn't read the review threads).
- **Caveat:** a maintainer's count with no stated method (likely a search of `lib`, `doc` and `test`), in one codebase, in 2017.
- **For the post:** a measured snapshot of one large codebase: 96.8% declarations (2,344 of 2,421) before any rule. The maintainers cited the TDZ as a reason to prefer declarations, the hazard the `const` style introduces.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/node-pr-12711.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/node-commit-aea7269c.json`

### 7.5 Node.js and `no-use-before-define` (2017–2019): a bug only the tests caught, and functions exempt because hoisting makes them "safe". ANECDOTAL (PRs)

- **Citation:** nodejs/node PR #14032 (Vse Mozhe Buty, opened 1 Jul 2017, landed as `5100cc68`); PR #27366 (Jiawen Geng, 23 Apr 2019, closed unmerged).
- **What it says:** #14032 began with a commit where `const common` was used before its `require`: "All tests have failed except linter." The rule was enabled with classes checked; "I've set the function option off as this is considered safe due to hoisting." Variables were exempted from inner-scope checks because "we have many cases with cross-references from callbacks which are rather difficult to refactor." In #27366, a proposal to stop checking classes for consistency ("Mind burden."), Ruben Bridgewater replied: "Functions may be used before definition without any problems." and "I think it's good to keep the rule as it is".
- **For the post:** the real-world bug `no-use-before-define` caught was a same-scope `const` used too early, the TDZ case. Node.js kept that check and exempted the two cases where reordering costs most: hoisted functions and references from inside callbacks.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/node-pr-14032.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/node-pr-27366.json`

### 7.6 Deno rejected a switch to top-level arrows in 2020: "There is nothing wrong with `function`." ANECDOTAL (PR review)

- **Citation:** denoland/deno PR #5934 "[WIP] Refactor: Export function as arrow function const" (opened 29 May 2020, closed 1 Jun 2020); review by Kitson Kelly (`kitsonk`), 29 May 2020; comments by Nayeem Rahman and Bartek Iwańczuk.
- **What it says:** the contributor argued arrows avoid "cost of function isolation like separate `this`". Kelly: "Use of arrow functions at the top level can run into TDZ issues," "which is why we chose the style guide we chose." "While that problem is rare, it is still a potential problem." Arrows also prevent "binding and calling with other `this` scopes, which could be surprising." "There is nothing wrong with `function`." Rahman: "the style guide clearly disallows this". Iwańczuk closed it: "this change is in contradiction to our style guide."
- **For the post:** the TDZ argument from a runtime maintainer, in the opposite direction to the maintainer's reason (3): the `const` style creates a new ordering hazard rather than removing one.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-pr-5934.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-pr-5934-reviews.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-pr-5934-review-comments.json`

### 7.7 Deno's std converted its top-level arrows in 2023 and broke `std/crypto`, which the tests didn't catch. ANECDOTAL (PRs)

- **Citation:** denoland/std PR #3687 "refactor: remove top-level arrow sytnax use" (Asher Gomez, opened 4 Oct 2023, merged 11 Oct 2023); PRs #6558 (merged 15 Apr 2025) and #6643 (7 May 2025, closed).
- **What it says:** the PR applied the style guide's rule (4.14) across std. Jeremy Banks: "I believe there are a couple problems with this change as currently written," "though they unfortunately aren't caught by the test suite." "The change breaks the re-export of the built-in crypto methods" (the old code exported bound methods; Banks showed the output). Gomez: "I've reverted the breaking changes to `std/crypto`." In 2025 std added a `no-top-level-arrow-syntax` lint rule (#6558), then tried to remove it after the docs dropped the section, "This rule no longer applies." (#6643); Yoshiya Hinosawa: "I added back this rule to the style guide".
- **For the post:** a real conversion between the two styles, done by hand by maintainers following their own guide, changed behaviour, and the test suite didn't notice. It's the case for Unicorn's refusal to autofix (4.9) and the risk any agent-driven conversion runs.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-std-pr-3687.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-std-pr-6558.json`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/deno-std-pr-6643.json`

### 7.8 Vue's contributing guide: "code readability is subjective", so no stylistic refactors. ANECDOTAL (project doc)

- **Citation:** vuejs/core `.github/contributing.md` at `24c9d0bf`.
- **What it says:** "we discourage contributors from submitting code refactors that are largely stylistic." "The reason is that code readability is subjective." Style changes "result in massive diffs" and make tracing behaviour changes harder. Vue's `eslint.config.js` sets none of the function-style rules.
- **For the post:** a counterpoint to enforcing a function style after the fact: one large project's maintainers refuse style-only rewrites because the diff costs more than the style gains.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/vue-contributing.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/vue-core-eslint.config.js`

### 7.9 What other large projects' own lint configs set (Oct 2026). VERIFIED (config source); ANECDOTAL as evidence of preference

- **Citation:** facebook/react `.eslintrc.js` (`c5ec5c6d`), babel/babel `eslint.config.ts` (`f0508530`), prettier/prettier `eslint.config.js` (`c7a823c4`), sveltejs/svelte `eslint.config.js` and `CONTRIBUTING.md` (`fe21fce0`), microsoft/TypeScript wiki "Coding guidelines" (fetched 29 Oct 2026).
- **What it says:** React: `func-style` off (`[OFF, 'declaration']`), `func-names` off, `no-use-before-define` off. Babel: `@typescript-eslint/no-use-before-define` with `functions: false`, `classes: false`, `allowNamedExports: true`. Prettier: `arrow-body-style` `as-needed`, `prefer-arrow-callback` on, `unicorn/consistent-function-scoping` off. Svelte: none of these, and its contributing guide covers naming only. TypeScript's contributor guidelines: "Use arrow functions over anonymous function expressions." with the warning "This is _**NOT**_ a prescriptive guideline for the TypeScript community."
- **Caveat:** settings only; I didn't count which style each codebase actually uses (that's for the corpus harness).
- **For the post:** across React, Babel, Prettier, Svelte, Vue, webpack (4.16) and Node.js (4.15), none requires `const` functions, and the order rule is off, unset, or exempts functions in every one (webpack's own repo turns off the rule its shared config enables).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/src/projects/react-eslintrc.js`, `babel-eslint.config.ts`, `prettier-eslint.config.js`, `svelte-eslint.config.js`, `svelte-CONTRIBUTING.md`, `typescript-wiki-Coding-guidelines.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/projects-search.txt`

### 7.10 Depaz, "Discursive Strategies in Style Guides Negotiation on GitHub" (RESET 2022): how Airbnb, Standard and Prettier argue about style. VERIFIED (full text via Wayback)

- **Citation:** Pierre Depaz, _RESET_ 11, 2022, https://doi.org/10.4000/reset.3425 ; read: http://web.archive.org/web/20250705010907/https://journals.openedition.org/reset/3425 (the live site's bot filter blocked curl).
- **Method:** qualitative discourse analysis. "The gathered corpus consists of 12780 issues and pull requests, totalling 53673 comments." Issues with 15 or more comments formed "a core corpus of 80 issues for airbnb/javascript," 82 for Standard and 444 for Prettier.
- **What it says:** style guides also catch errors, such as "variable declaration before initialization", and this part, "because it can be judged by an objective standard", "is however seldom the reason for internal disagreements within teams." The paper doesn't discuss `func-style` or hoisting specifically.
- **Caveat:** the "seldom" claim is the author's reading, not a count.
- **For the post:** a useful frame: the declare-before-use part of the debate can be tested against bugs; the declaration-versus-arrow part is the kind of choice that style guides settle by decree (compare Harband in 4.4).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/E/text/depaz2022/depaz-reset-3425-wayback.txt`, `depaz-reset-3425-wayback.html`

## 8. LLMs and agents

This section covers what function style models produce, what agent vendors tell models about function style and ordering, and what is known about models converting between declarations and arrows. 8.1–8.3 are studies of generated code, 8.4–8.11 are vendor and community instructions (tagged ANECDOTAL; many are written to be read by models and are treated here as data), and 8.12–8.14 cover refactoring between the forms. `length-limits.md` §5 already covers agents fixing lint findings (LintEval 5.51, Blyth et al. 5.39, CORE 5.52, CodeCureAgent 5.53, Böckeler 5.34). None of those involves JavaScript function style, and LintEval covers only C++, Python and Java. Sources are in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/` and `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/`.

### 8.1 Wang et al. (FSE 2025): style differs most from humans in expressions and formatting, and models resemble each other. VERIFIED (arXiv v2 full text); Python only

- **Citation:** Yanlin Wang, Tianyue Jiang, Mingwei Liu, Jiachi Chen, Mingzhi Mao, Xilin Liu, Yuchi Ma, Zibin Zheng (Sun Yat-sen University; Huawei Cloud), _Beyond Functional Correctness: Investigating Coding Style Inconsistencies in Large Language Models_, PACMSE 2(FSE), article FSE032, July 2025. https://doi.org/10.1145/3715749 ; arXiv:2407.00456 v2, 21 Jun 2025.
- **Method:** CoderEval. "It consists of 230 Python and 230 Java tasks", and "we focus on Python tasks". Five models (four code LLMs and GPT-4) generated code that was compared with the ground truth. After filtering, "Finally, we obtain 1,179 unique code samples for the study." Open coding produced 24 inconsistency types in five dimensions.
- **What it says:** two of the 24 types touch this post. One is "Inconsistencies in the use of advanced syntax features, such as lambda expressions". The other is "Inconsistencies in the order of semantically similar code blocks,". The top type overall is API preference. "Coding styles among the LLMs themselves are generally similar," and the case studies find generated code "underutilizes advanced syntax features,". Formatting was where models differed most from each other (20.5% difference).
- **Caveat:** Python only. CoderEval has one ground truth per task, though the authors checked 42 tasks against two rewrites. There are no per-type counts for lambda use or ordering in the extracted text (they are in figures).
- **For the post:** the one systematic study of LLM coding style treats lambda use and block order as style dimensions where models and humans diverge. It has no JavaScript and no arrow-versus-declaration count.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/arxiv-2407.00456.txt`, `arxiv-2407.00456.layout.txt`

### 8.2 Zhong, Zou and Adams (arXiv 2025–26): ESLint on ChatGPT's JavaScript finds undefined variables, not function style. VERIFIED (arXiv v2 full text)

- **Citation:** Suzhen Zhong, Ying Zou, Bram Adams (Queen's University), _Developer-LLM Conversations: An Empirical Study of Interactions and Generated Code Quality_, arXiv:2509.10402 v2, 28 Aug 2026.
- **Method:** CodeChat, 587,568 coding conversations filtered from WildChat (ChatGPT, Apr 2023–Jul 2025), holding 1.7 million code snippets. "For JavaScript, we use ESLint", with Pylint, PMD, Cppcheck and a C# analyzer for the other four languages.
- **What it says:** "undefined variables appear in 75.3% of first-turn JavaScript snippets." "unused variables occur in 33.7%," and syntax errors in 14.4%. In multi-turn JavaScript conversations, the top issues stayed statistically unchanged across turns.
- **Caveat:** snippets in chat answers often reference code the user has, so undefined variables are partly an artefact. The ESLint configuration isn't given in the text I read, and no function-style rule appears in the results. I didn't find the JavaScript snippet count in the text.
- **For the post:** the one large ESLint run on model-written JavaScript found reports nothing about `func-style`, `prefer-arrow-callback` or `no-use-before-define`. Whether models favour arrows is unmeasured (8.3).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/text/arxiv-2509.10402.txt`

### 8.3 No study measures arrow functions against declarations in model-written JavaScript or TypeScript. Negative finding

- **Searched and read:** Wang et al. (8.1, Python), Zhong et al. (8.2, no function-style rules), _code_transformed_ (Xu et al., arXiv:2506.12014 v2: Python naming and complexity in GitHub repositories, no JavaScript), IFEvalCode (arXiv:2507.22462 v2: has JavaScript and TypeScript tasks with style constraints, but no arrow or declaration constraint found in the text), and `length-limits.md` 5.2–5.5 (Python and Java size metrics). Web searches on arrow functions in LLM-generated code and on AI-code detection features found nothing JavaScript-specific about function form.
- **What does exist:** vendor example code that models are given (8.4) and vendor examples of instructions (8.5–8.11).
- **For the post:** whether agents tend to write declarations or arrows unprompted is an open question. The corpus harness could measure it by counting `FunctionDeclaration` against `VariableDeclarator` with an arrow or function-expression initialiser in agent-authored pull requests.

### 8.4 Vercel's React agent skill writes declarations: 149 against 19 `const` arrows in its examples. ANECDOTAL (my count of a vendor document at a pinned commit); Vendor (Vercel)

- **Citation:** vercel-labs/agent-skills, `skills/react-best-practices/AGENTS.md` at `1a2c79d4e6c4a2283dc2c614b6538cbe1ed5ca62`.
- **Method (my count, 29 Oct 2026):** across the 170 `ts`/`tsx`/`js`/`jsx` code blocks, lines starting a function declaration (149) against lines starting `const` or `let` assigned an arrow (19) or a function expression (0). It is a regular-expression count at line starts, so it includes nested functions and misses one-line callbacks. The script is `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/check/count_function_style.py`.
- **What it says:** the skill gives no instruction on function form. Its "hoist" headings, such as "Hoist Static I/O to Module Level", mean moving work to module scope, not JavaScript declaration hoisting.
- **Caveat:** one document, and a crude count.
- **For the post:** an agent that learns from this skill's examples writes code that `func-style: expression` rejects about 149 times. Vendor example code and a lint preset can pull an agent in opposite directions.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/vercel/react-best-practices-AGENTS-1a2c79d4e6c4a2283dc2c614b6538cbe1ed5ca62.md`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/vercel/function-style-counts.txt`

### 8.5 GitHub Copilot docs: "Use arrow functions for callbacks", next to a `function` declaration example. ANECDOTAL (github/docs at a pinned commit); Vendor (GitHub/Microsoft)

- **Citation:** github/docs at `6cbe6413ac7177bb692a78c171a74ae8be50d721` (29 Oct 2026): `data/reusables/copilot/custom-instructions-effective.md` (in its current path since 27 Oct 2025) and `content/copilot/tutorials/customize-code-review.md` (in its current path since 14 Apr 2026).
- **What it says:** the example instructions file lists "Use function based components in React." and "Use arrow functions for callbacks." The code review tutorial's TypeScript example says "Use arrow functions for callbacks and short functions". Its "Prefer" example in the same file is a declaration, `function processData(data: DataShape): string`. Instructions "should be short, self-contained statements".
- **Caveat:** example content, not a GitHub style rule.
- **For the post:** the vendor example scopes arrows to callbacks and short functions and still uses a declaration for a named function. That is narrower than `func-style: expression`, which rejects every declaration.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/ghdocs/data_reusables_copilot_custom-instructions-effective.md`, `content_copilot_tutorials_customize-code-review.md`, `ccr-history.txt`, `cie-history.txt`

### 8.6 github/awesome-copilot: no JavaScript or TypeScript instruction file says anything about arrow functions or declarations. ANECDOTAL (files at pinned commits); negative

- **Citation:** github/awesome-copilot at `5269f7ea2d6c3a9bfda5ed878b6726cf509121fd`: 15 JS/TS-related `instructions/*.instructions.md` files read (Next.js, Node/Vitest, Astro, Svelte, Vue, TanStack, NestJS, Playwright, MCP server, Azure Functions, PCF React, Copilot SDK, generic code review, object calisthenics). Also the last versions of `reactjs.instructions.md` (`4b9e3f2a`) and `typescript-5-es2022.instructions.md` (`b8a80c5a`), both removed in `b1f3346e` (13 Mar 2026, "content review").
- **What it says:** nothing on arrow against declaration, hoisting or file order. The nearest are "Prefer functions over classes" (Node/Vitest) and "Functional components with hooks as default" (the removed React file). Code samples are mostly arrows passed as callbacks.
- **For the post:** in the largest public collection of Copilot instructions, nobody wrote a function-form rule. Where projects want one, they use a linter.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/awesome-copilot/` (all files), `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/awesome-copilot-tree-5269f7ea2d6c3a9bfda5ed878b6726cf509121fd.txt`

### 8.7 Anthropic: leave inferable conventions out of CLAUDE.md, give Claude a linter, and keep preferences out of hooks. ANECDOTAL (official docs); Vendor (Anthropic)

- **Citation:** code.claude.com, _Best practices for Claude Code_ (`/docs/en/best-practices.md`) and _How Claude remembers your project_ (`/docs/en/memory.md`). platform.claude.com, _Prompting best practices_, "Overeagerness". All read 29 Oct 2026. Cross-reference `length-limits.md` 5.25–5.26.
- **What it says:** "Only write down what Claude can't find by reading the repo,". Models "find your file layout, your conventions, your test framework," on their own. A good check is something that comes back pass or fail, and the list of examples includes a linter. Some rules must be enforced: "enforce the rules that can't have exceptions." A CLAUDE.md line "works most of the time, not every time." The reverse also holds: "if you have hooks for things that are only preferences," "delete them and let Claude decide." The prompting guide's sample says "A bug fix doesn't need surrounding code cleaned up."
- **Caveat:** nothing in these pages addresses function form or order.
- **For the post:** the vendor's model is that a consistent codebase teaches its own style, and a linter is the deterministic check. Function form is the kind of preference the docs would leave to Claude, unless the project makes it a lint error. The bug-fix line is in tension with a rule that flags every declaration in a file an agent touches.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/anthropic/claude-code-best-practices.md`, `claude-code-memory.md`, `claude-prompting-best-practices.md`

### 8.8 OpenAI: match the codebase, don't fix untouched lines, and leave lint to CI. ANECDOTAL (cookbook and docs at pinned commits); Vendor (OpenAI; Anysphere for the Cursor prompt)

- **Citation:** openai/openai-cookbook at `f5819076643583e2c2a77268e7fe522e927499d0`: `examples/gpt-5/gpt-5_prompting_guide.ipynb` and `examples/gpt-5/codex_prompting_guide.ipynb`. developers.openai.com, _Custom instructions with AGENTS.md_ (Codex), read 29 Oct 2026. openai/codex at `55c099a142eb302188fee00ab9e9062f2db1c359` has no root AGENTS.md, and its nested ones are about Rust.
- **What it says:**
  - GPT-5 guide: "model-written code should adhere to existing style and design standards," and "'blend in' to the codebase as neatly as possible". Its sample coding guidelines say "Keep changes consistent with the style of the existing codebase." and "do not fix pre-existing errors on lines you didn't touch."
  - Codex guide: "Conform to the codebase conventions: follow existing patterns, helpers, naming, formatting," and "if you must diverge, state why."
  - Cursor's tuned prompt, quoted in the GPT-5 guide: "Do not produce code-golf or overly clever one-liners unless explicitly requested." Cursor found GPT-5's tool-call code "sometimes hard to read due to terseness, with single-letter variable names dominant."
  - Codex AGENTS.md docs: the example asks for `npm run lint` before a PR, and review rules should "reserve formatting and lint checks for CI".
- **Caveat:** prompts and guidance, not measurements, except Cursor's one-line observation.
- **For the post:** both vendors tell the model to copy the file's existing style and not to fix untouched lines. A rule that flags every declaration in a mixed file puts blending in and passing lint in conflict. Cursor's warning about terse code is the counterweight to concise arrow bodies (maintainer reason 1).
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/openai/gpt-5_prompting_guide-f5819076643583e2c2a77268e7fe522e927499d0.txt`, `codex_prompting_guide-f5819076643583e2c2a77268e7fe522e927499d0.txt`, `developers-codex-guides-agents-md.md`, `codex-docs-agents_md-55c099a142eb302188fee00ab9e9062f2db1c359.md`

### 8.9 Cursor docs: "Use a linter instead" of copying style guides into rules. ANECDOTAL (official docs); Vendor (Anysphere)

### 8.11 A popular community agent skill tells agents to follow the stepdown rule. ANECDOTAL (skill file at a pinned commit)

- **Citation:** sickn33/antigravity-awesome-skills, `plugins/agentic-awesome-skills/skills/clean-code/SKILL.md` at `753c5f7bb64650b7d3ac0ab84d9b24892d7aed8b` (repository created 14 Jan 2026, 48,748 stars on 29 Oct 2026).
- **What it says:** "**The Stepdown Rule**: We want the code to read like a top-down narrative." and "**The Newspaper Metaphor**: High-level concepts at the top, details at the bottom."
- **Caveat:** stars aren't installs. The skill paraphrases Clean Code without page references.
- **For the post:** agents are being told callers-first order. In a codebase with `func-style: expression` and `no-use-before-define`, that instruction produces lint errors, because a `const` function has to be defined above its first use. The opposite case holds as well: a hoisting-free codebase forces callees-first, which this skill calls wrong.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/skills-community/sickn33-clean-code-SKILL-753c5f7bb64650b7d3ac0ab84d9b24892d7aed8b.md`

### 8.12 What a safe conversion to an arrow must check: ESLint's fixer and Lebab both refuse when `this`, `arguments`, recursion or generators are involved. VERIFIED (ESLint source at v10.13.0; Lebab README at a pinned commit)

- **Citations:** eslint/eslint `lib/rules/prefer-arrow-callback.js` at `v10.13.0`. lebab/lebab `README.md` at `2d620cc390038f10223cdf480a71e8abc8e4ed70`.
- **What they say:** ESLint's rule skips generators, named functions that refer to themselves ("Skip recursive functions.") and functions that use `arguments` ("Skip if it's using arguments."). Its autofix bails when the callback uses `this` without `.bind(this)`, when parameters are duplicated (a syntax error in arrows), and when the first parameter is named `this`. Lebab lists its `arrow` transform as a safe one whose output "should be almost 100% equivalent of the original code." It is "not applied to unbound functions that use `this`" or to "functions that use `arguments`", and has a known limitation that it "can mess up prototype-based classes".
- **Caveat:** these cover callbacks only. `func-style` itself has no autofix in ESLint (another helper covers the rule). Converting a top-level declaration also changes hoisting: a call above the new `const` throws in the temporal dead zone. Neither tool handles that case.
- **For the post:** the deterministic tools list exactly what an agent has to check before turning a `function` into an arrow, and they decline the hard cases. An agent fixing `func-style` has no such guard unless tests or `no-use-before-define` catch the mistake.
- **Text:** `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/eslint/prefer-arrow-callback-v10.13.0.js`, `scratchpad/func-style-lit/D/src/lebab/README-2d620cc390038f10223cdf480a71e8abc8e4ed70.md`

### 8.13 Cross-reference: agents and lint fixes in `length-limits.md` §5. Not re-verified

- **LintEval (`length-limits.md` 5.51):** models clear up to 90.4% of alerts but break behaviour in over 31% of samples when no tests are in the loop. C++, Python and Java only. **CORE (`length-limits.md` 5.52):** analyzer-passing fixes can still change behaviour. **CodeCureAgent (`length-limits.md` 5.53):** re-running the analyzer was the main guard. **Blyth et al. (`length-limits.md` 5.39):** fixed lint issues get re-introduced. **Böckeler (`length-limits.md` 5.34):** an agent under ESLint size rules complied by splitting.
- **For the post:** none of these includes a JavaScript function-style rule. The general finding to carry over is that lint-driven edits without tests sometimes break behaviour, and a declaration-to-`const` conversion is a case where the break (TDZ, `this`, `arguments`) is semantic and silent until run.

### 8.14 Agents converting declarations to arrows, or breaking hoisting while doing it: no study and no primary report found. Negative finding

- **Searched:** `gh search issues` in anthropics/claude-code and openai/codex for `func-style`, `arrow function`, `prefer-arrow-callback`, `convert function declarations to arrow functions` and `function declaration hoisting`. No relevant issue was found (matches were unrelated). Web searches for LLM ES5-to-ES6 refactoring of arrow functions and `this` found only tutorials and a 2021 ES-modules refactoring paper (Paltoglou et al., arXiv:2107.10164, not about arrows).
- **For the post:** if the post wants a number, it has to come from the agent harness, for example a file mixing hoisted declarations, `this`-using methods and `arguments`, under `func-style: expression` and `no-use-before-define`, with tests that catch TDZ errors.

## 9. Ultracite's own history

9.1 is the timeline from `git log -S` over everything but `apps/`, `research/` and the changelog (search strings: `func-style`, `allowArrowFunctions`, `no-use-before-define`, `func-names`, `prefer-arrow-callback`, `arrow-body-style`, `consistent-function-scoping`, `function-component-definition`, `useArrowFunction`, `noInvalidUseBeforeDeclaration`, `useConsistentArrowReturn`, `noFunctionAssign`), `packages/cli/CHANGELOG.md`, and read-only `gh api search/issues` on haydenbleasel/ultracite for `func-style`, `arrow function`, `arrow functions`, `function declaration`, `function expression`, `hoist`, `hoisting`, `use-before-define`, `use before define`, `before initialization`, each Biome rule name, `TanStack route` and `createFileRoute`. Results in `scratchpad/func-style-lit/main/src/ultracite/` (diffs, `git-log-S.txt`, copies of the current config files) and `scratchpad/func-style-lit/main/src/gh/`. 9.2–9.5 are the recorded reasons and incidents, 9.6 what the presets set now (worktree at `7bf1b7be`, 29 Oct 2026), 9.7 what each preset reports on the same small files, 9.8 what Ultracite tells agents, 9.9 the public record of users, 9.10 Hayden's account.

Base `no-use-before-define` `"off"` under the comment "ESLint Disabled for Typescript-ESLint", and `@typescript-eslint/no-use-before-define` `"error"`. `.js` files were ignored (`ignorePatterns: ["**/*.js"]`). No comment on any function-style rule. |

Biome has no rule that reports function declarations, so from here until V7 Ultracite enforced no declaration-versus-expression style at all. |

- **No commit, issue or changelog entry gives a reason for preferring expressions.** The 2022 setting has no comment; the V7 and July 2026 settings were copies (the V7 Oxlint value, then a commit to "match oxlint's no-void and func-style options"). The only written reasoning about these rules is the TanStack override (9.4).
- **For about a year and a half (Jul 2024 to Dec 2025), Ultracite didn't enforce the style at all**, because the Biome-only presets had nothing like `func-style`. Biome users still don't (9.6).
- **For the post:** the setting is a preference held since the first commit. Its reason has to come from Hayden (9.10). Keep dates and preset names out of the post.
